Engineering Physical AI

18 Engineering Physical AI

Lecture slides for this chapter

Purpose

Physical AI stack focus for this chapter.

Why does a better model not, by itself, make a better machine?

The learned model is the part of a physical machine that is easiest to improve and easiest to score, so it attracts most of the effort. Useful service depends on everything around it as well. The body must sustain the task across loads, surfaces, and hours of duty. The learned behavior must hold in the states its own actions create. Each proposal must be admitted, corrected, or refused before the motion it asks for is already under way. The evidence for all of it must match the conditions of operation and stay true as the machine and its site change. Each of these can be engineered well in isolation while the machine as a whole remains unjustified, because doubt accumulates across every premise a physical claim depends on, and a premise that no one names or owns is assumed silently. A team that cannot join these parts into one argument keeps improving the model while an unmeasured floor, an unqualified detector, or an unowned restriction decides what the machine actually does. The same joined argument also chooses what to build next, since the most valuable measurement, experience, or design change is the one that retires the weakest open premise. At the causal boundary, irreversibility makes that weakest premise, not the best prediction, the measure of what a machine may be trusted to do.

Learning Objectives
  • Explain why a better prediction can still produce a worse physical action, and synthesize the four laws into the design argument that answers it
  • Distinguish an unobserved premise from a monitored unknown, and explain why only a standing restriction can contain an unobserved premise
  • Apply the four laws to select and evaluate a candidate operating mode for an unfamiliar machine
  • Construct residual-claims entries with supported restrictions, accountable owners, and predeclared closure tests
  • Evaluate the throughput cost of an operating restriction against the cost of the closure campaign that would retire it
  • Select the next measurement, learning experiment, or design change needed to extend a machine’s capability
Explore the concepts in this chapter

The volume’s concept map, opened on this chapter. Select a concept to read how it connects to ideas in other chapters. Open the full map ↗

18.1 From a Better Prediction to Useful Service

On this book’s own release and approval records (Deployment Release and Responsible Physical AI), the warehouse mobile manipulator is refused. Even the stipulated evidence branch earns it only conditional service, held in place by premises that no sensor on the machine reports, such as the friction of the aisle floor. Improving its learned models alone would not change that verdict while the supporting physical and timing premises remain unqualified. That refusal is the engineering form of the answer to the question The Causal Boundary opened with: why a better prediction can still produce a worse physical action, and what the rest of the machine must supply so that a model’s proposals become useful work within justified limits.

The question began with a contrast. Traditional machine learning evaluated advisory predictions against static datasets, and agentic machine learning could run iterative reasoning loops inside controlled digital sandboxes, behind glass, with elastic time, transactional rollback, and symbolic interfaces. Those privileges depended on the sandbox; external calls could already produce irreversible effects. Physical AI stepped through that glass into the continuous world, where computation commands actuators across the causal boundary. That step exposed computation to continuous time, in which delay becomes distance traveled, and to mechanical and thermodynamic irreversibility, which forbids undo. The illustrated feedback workload also required one action at a time under a real-time deadline, a batch size of one (\(\BatchSize=1\)). This scheduling constraint does not prohibit coordinated axes or batching elsewhere in the system. These constraints forced an architectural response, the separation of speculative proposals from deterministic permission, which is a design choice rather than a fact of physics.

One reference platform carried the argument: a warehouse mobile manipulator that picks an object from a moving conveyor, opens a spring-latched cage door, and carries the payload down an aisle shared with people. That mission exercised every capability the book develops (sizing the physical and computational dynamics, teaching learned policies, integrating a real-time runtime, evaluating closed-loop behavior, and maintaining the operating envelope as the machine and its site degrade). Each design choice constrained the next.

Predictive accuracy alone does not establish the operating premises on which physical success depends. What an actuator command does once it crosses the causal boundary depends on the distance a mobile base travels while a neural network runs its forward pass, the friction available at the tire-floor interface during hard braking, the states into which the robot’s own prior actions drive it, and the gaps between demonstration datasets and live site conditions. Improving a model’s statistical accuracy can refine the proposed trajectory, and shortening inference can recover timing margin, but neither result by itself qualifies traction, closed-loop competence, or the complete stopping budget.

The second half of the question, what the surrounding machine must supply, resolves into the four-step argument of The Question This Book Answers, pairing one engineering discipline with each of the four laws. Under the first law (irreversibility), the body’s mechanical and thermal limits bound what any proposal may request. Part I translated payload capacity, surface traction, contact forces, motor heating, and energy reserves into strict operating budgets that govern proposal horizons, execution cadence, and deterministic fallback paths. Under the second law (endogenous data), policy competence holds only over the states induced by closed-loop interaction. Part II tied proposer evaluation directly to the states the physical machine actually visits, turning recovery failures into targeted data-collection runs rather than unhandled anomalies. Under the third law (proposal is not permission), execution authority resides in an independent permission path rather than the learned model. Part III joined observation freshness contracts, grounded geometric targets, timed trajectories, and real-time admission filters onto a compute layout built to meet hard deadlines under load. Under the fourth law (permission must be earned), deployment scope extends only as far as qualified system-wide evidence supports it. Part IV structured physical operation as a governed service contract that separates proposer autonomy, whole-system task success, and protective intervention. A least-change speed filter, for example, can preserve an in-flight delivery even when a raw proposal requires intervention, whereas an emergency stop protects physical hardware without fulfilling the promised service.

The release verdict is where the four laws meet. In the baseline audit of Adjudicating the Safety Case, the book’s illustrative records yield REFUSE, because tail risks and fault margins remain unverified. The stipulated branch of Worked restricted service with stipulated evidence shows which evidence would raise that verdict to conditional authorization (COND). Under those stipulated performance and fault records, the machine may run inspected-route transport, guarded door approaches, and close-proximity handovers, provided that standing conditions on floor traction, pedestrian crossings, feeder alignment, and sensor calibration stay verified. Each condition traces back through the record chain to a measured limit, an evaluated behavior, or a timed check, which is what separates a result stated under premises from a deployment claim.

Assembled, these parts close one causal loop through the physical world (figure 1). The loop runs at rates four orders of magnitude apart: the Brain proposes at 2 Hz, the permission path on the discrete safety microcontroller admits, corrects, or refuses at 1 kHz, and the drives close their current loops at 20 kHz. The loop closes only through the World, and the World returns only what its sensors capture, with capture times, to the permission path and to the Brain. A state the sensors never carry, such as the friction under a clear film, is invisible to every level at once. Governance surrounds all five levels and changes on the timescale of releases, so it sets the budgets and standing conditions that the fast loop enforces without taking part in any single control cycle.

The records summarized in table 1 bring the four laws into one engineering workflow. Rather than treating safety as an abstract property of a model, each part of the architecture leaves behind durable, auditable records that preserve the evidence and operating intent when tasks, payloads, or site conditions change.

The synthesis also completes the history traced in From Prediction to Action. Over eight decades, learning moved from the machine’s periphery toward its actuators. Deterministic mechanisms gave way to learned perception feeding analytical planners, then to end-to-end motor policies, and now to foundation models that propose action chunks across many robot bodies. At each step, a guarantee that physical separation or a watchful person once supplied had to move inside the machine.

The warehouse platform shows where that history now stands. Its split between an intent model proposing at 2 Hz and a chunk policy renewed every 50 ms mirrors current vision-language-action pipelines, and the guarantees those statistical models displaced survive in the deterministic architecture around them. The reflex halt beneath Shakey’s STRIPS planner and the lower layers of Brooks’s subsumption architecture, which kept running while higher layers were busy or wrong, have become the discrete permission path, enforcing signed kinematic and dynamic envelopes sized for this body. The remote operators who stayed close to the loop at the DARPA Robotics Challenge have become a formal intervention authority with timed handover budgets, and the environmental oversight a person once supplied has become explicit standing conditions of release.

The next phase, taken up in section 18.4.3, brings foundation models that control many bodies while learning continuously from fleet deployments. Cross-embodiment pretraining broadens what a model can propose, and it also carries the model further from the empirical distribution of any single site. Wider proposing latitude therefore makes the independent permission path more necessary, not less. It demands stronger checks at runtime and more defensible evidence at release, an architectural response to the safety-limit and conformity requirements of European machinery law (The standards map for the running machine).

The architecture is a method meant to carry across platforms. Its transferable core consists of the foundational questions, dynamic budgets, test protocols, and interface boundaries; the platform-specific elements are the calibrated numerical thresholds, kinematic envelopes, and actuator limits. The rest of this chapter tests how that core transfers to an unfamiliar packaging cell (section 18.2), tracks the premises no runtime sensor can check in a residual-claims register (section 18.3), and uses those open premises to select the next capability to build (section 18.4).

18.2 Generalizing to Unfamiliar Systems

An automated packaging cell that shuttles containers, seats closure tabs, and repeats high-rate cycles across a full shift can ram a closing gate, crush a carton wall, or bake its motor windings if its learned proposers outrun its physical limits. Building it requires the same pairing as the warehouse machine: a capable learned proposer with an execution pipeline whose runtime authority respects kinematic reach, contact force, and thermal buildup in the drive motors. The four bedrock laws trace one chain through such a machine. The chain runs from the task’s irreversible consequences to measurable physical envelopes, through the closed-loop data that trains and validates its policies, to the real-time runtime that gates motor commands, and finally to the operating envelope certified for deployment. Auditing a machine the book has not yet studied tests whether that chain is a transferable method or only a description of one robot.

↩︎ The four bedrock laws, from irreversibility to permission must be earned, are established in The Four Bedrock Laws.

18.2.1 Auditing a new machine with the four laws

Auditing an unfamiliar machine out of order (evaluating a learned policy before the stopping budget fixes the speed ceiling, for example) wastes the evaluation on states the permission path will never admit. The audit therefore asks one question per law, in the order the laws constrain one another, and each answer leaves a record behind:

  1. Irreversibility. What can the task do that no later command can undo, and what measured bound keeps it from happening? The answer names the collisions, plastic deformations, and thermal overloads of the task and sizes the envelope that excludes them, such as a speed ceiling from the stopping budget. It produces the machine’s limit records.
  2. Endogenous data. Do the demonstrations and the closed-loop evaluation cover the states the machine will enter once that envelope is enforced? A limit added in step 1 changes the states the proposer sees, so the evaluation must be run inside the envelope, not before it. It produces the provenance record and the evaluation record.
  3. Proposal is not permission. Which independent path admits, corrects, or refuses each proposal, on which sensors, within which timing budget, and with which fallback when permission lapses? It produces the enforcement record and the timing budget the permission path must meet.
  4. Permission must be earned. Which operating claims does the assembled evidence support, under which standing conditions, who answers for each decision behind them, and which premises remain open? It produces the release manifest, the approval record, and the residual-claims register entries for every premise left open.

Because each step constrains the next, adjustments ripple back up the list. Reducing the permitted transit speed to satisfy a stopping budget in step 1 alters the visual flow and dynamic states that step 2 must evaluate. Tracing those four steps on a high-rate packaging cell shows how a single limit in step 1 reshapes the data, runtime checks, and release claims that follow.

18.2.2 A packaging cell across the three machine classes

Consider a packaging cell in a logistics hub, laid out as in figure 2 (panel a). A carriage carrying one carton runs along a straight track, driven by a linear servo drive at the track’s near end. Partway along, an insertion head presses down on the carton’s flaps to seat its retention tabs. At the far end, a gate closes across the track whenever the next station cannot accept a carton, and a carriage still moving when the gate closes drives its carton into the gate and the frame. The cell is laid out tightly for cycle rate, so the carriage is only a few centimeters from its stop line when a closure can begin. This single cell couples all three machine classes within one enclosure: the carriage as moving mass, the insertion head as contact force, and the drive as thermal dissipation, because it cycles the carriage through rapid accelerations all shift.

↩︎ The three machine classes (mass-dominated mobility, contact-dominated manipulation, and energy- and thermal-dominated operation) are established in Three Machine Classes.

International Electrotechnical Commission. 2007. IEC 60085:2007: Electrical Insulation, Thermal Evaluation and Designation. International Electrotechnical Commission.

The first law asks what the machine cannot undo, so the audit begins by cataloging the cell’s irreversible state changes alongside the governing physical bounds. A loaded parcel container moving along a linear transfer rail carries momentum. When a carriage strikes an obstruction, kinetic energy dissipates through plastic deformation of the container and structural deflection of the frame, and commanding the drive motor in reverse cannot undo that damage. The insertion head introduces a second irreversible boundary: an actuator drives a tool head against package tabs to lock retaining clips. If the actuator pushes harder than the carton wall can support in compression, the container buckles and crushes its contents. The high-duty drive introduces a third irreversible boundary through Joule heating (\(\WindingLossPower = \TorqueProducingCurrent^2 \ElectricalResistance(\WindingTemperature)\)). Continuous high-acceleration cycles pump heat into sealed motor windings. Winding insulation does not need to melt to fail; elevated temperatures speed up chemical degradation over time, shortening insulation life rapidly (International Electrotechnical Commission 2007). Cooling the housing afterward cannot restore the dielectric strength already lost. Because insulation damage accumulates across duty cycles, an instantaneous temperature trip threshold alone cannot bound lifetime wear.

Because the carriage’s momentum is already committed, the permission path must act before the carriage consumes its clearance. The stop modeled next is the cell’s application-level path: a camera watches the gate, a learned model recognizes that a closure has begun, and the admitted stop command goes to the carriage brake. That path keeps normal operation clear of the gate, but it is not the cell’s certified protective function, which the third-law discussion below and Problem 1 take up. The constant-deceleration stopping relation of Kinematic stopping distance and pre-brake delay tests a candidate design for it, here on hypothetical scenario inputs rather than measurements from a commissioned cell.

Napkin Math 18.1: The delay wall
Problem: The loaded carriage travels at 1.50 m/s toward a gate whose fixed stop line is 60 mm away when the hazardous closure begins, maintaining constant speed until braking begins. Can it stop short of the line, and if not, what is the fastest speed whose stop fits once a 15 mm reserve is held back?

Variables:

  • Moving mass of carriage and loaded tray \(\Mass\) = 12 kg and nominal speed \(\SpeedAtDetection\) = 1.50 m/s (Illustrative)
  • Clearance 60 mm, less 15 mm reserved for uncertainty and residual clearance, leaves a stopping budget \(\StoppingDistanceBudget\) = 45 mm (Illustrative)
  • Braking lower bound \(\CredibleBrakingDeceleration\) = 15 m/s² after the delay, for whichever brake the stop command engages (Illustrative; a bound to qualify under load, pad wear, and rail conditions, not a motor’s peak rating)
  • Delay from closure to full braking \(\PrebrakeDelay\): 16 ms to a usable image, including frame phase and exposure, 36 ms for transfer, inference, scheduling, and admission, 2 ms for command delivery, and 10 ms for brake buildup, in all 64 ms (Illustrative upper bounds)

Math:

  1. Nominal stop (equation 2.2, margins held in the reserve): \(\StoppingDistance = \SpeedAtDetection\PrebrakeDelay + \SpeedAtDetection^2/2\CredibleBrakingDeceleration\) = 1.50 m/s \(\times\) 64 ms \(+\) (1.50 m/s)\(^2/(2 \times\) 15 m/s²) = 96 mm \(+\) 75 mm = 171 mm, a deficit of 111 mm.
  2. Speed ceiling (equation 2.3 with \(\StoppingDistanceBudget\) as the available distance): \(\CredibleBrakingDeceleration\PrebrakeDelay\) = 0.96 m/s and \(2\CredibleBrakingDeceleration{}\StoppingDistanceBudget\) = 1.35 m²/s², and the square root of (0.96 m/s)\(^2\) \(+\) 1.35 m²/s² is 1.51 m/s. The positive root is \(\SpeedBound\) = 1.51 m/s \(-\) 0.96 m/s = 0.55 m/s.
  3. Candidate mode at 0.50 m/s, below the ceiling: 32 mm of delay travel \(+\) 8.3 mm of braking = 40.3 mm, leaving 19.7 mm before the stop line. Translational energy \(\KineticEnergy = \Mass \SpeedAtDetection^2/2\) falls from 13.5 J to 1.5 J, a reduction of 88.9 percent.

Result: At nominal speed the carriage reaches the gate before braking even begins. The budget admits at most 0.55 m/s, and the 0.50 m/s candidate keeps more than the allocated reserve.

Systems insight: At these speeds delay travel, not braking, exhausts the clearance. The 64 ms chain alone carries the nominal carriage 96 mm, farther than the stop line, so the condition \(\StoppingDistance \le \ClearDistance\) of principle 1 is met first by lowering speed or shortening the chain from closure to braking. The carriage’s speed limit belongs to that whole chain, not to its brake alone.

The slower mode is therefore worth qualifying. Cutting speed to a third shrinks the two terms of the stop unequally (figure 2). Delay travel is linear in speed and falls threefold, from 96 mm to 32 mm; braking distance is quadratic and falls ninefold, to 8.3 mm. At the restricted speed the delay chain, not the brake, therefore sets most of the stop, which is why the next question is which premise moves the ceiling most.

The positive root also shows how the ceiling responds to each premise. Halving the processing allocation to 18 ms raises the ceiling to 0.66 m/s; because delay travel dominates this stop, reducing that allocation recovers substantial speed margin. Halving the qualified braking bound to 7.5 m/s² lowers the ceiling to 0.47 m/s, below the candidate 0.50 m/s mode, which then no longer fits. The two premises play different roles. Shorter latency is an opportunity for throughput, whereas a braking shortfall invalidates the mode outright, which is why the loaded deceleration must be qualified under worst-case conditions before the cell is commissioned. The stopping equation fixes the relationship; only measurements on the assembled cell can establish that the latency budget, the deceleration bound, and the reserve hold.

↩︎ This speed ceiling, the positive root of the stopping equation, is established for the mobile base in Dynamic velocity ceilings and clearance budgeting.

The second law evaluates the competence of the cell’s learned proposers in the closed loop. This packaging cell runs three distinct learned proposers. A vision model infers container contents to propose carriage transit speeds (fragile or liquid contents tolerate gentler accelerations and stops than solid goods). A multimodal network estimates cardboard thickness to propose insertion forces and dwell durations. An electro-thermal estimator proposes acceleration profiles to manage motor winding temperatures. Each proposer’s actions alter what it observes next. Throttling carriage speed changes camera frame timing and motion blur, extending tool dwell changes the material deformation that later thickness estimates read, and reshaping acceleration profiles changes the temperature trajectory the thermal estimator measures. Evidence of proposer competence therefore holds only for the state distribution sampled during closed-loop evaluation, and the question is whether that distribution matches the conditions admitted at runtime. A safety restriction imposed after training shifts that distribution. If closed-loop evaluation sampled only the nominal transit speed of 1.50 m/s, clamping speed to 0.50 m/s pushes the vision model into visual dynamics it never encountered during evaluation, so the restriction that secures the stopping distance also opens a regime without evidence. The engineering team must either evaluate the policy under derated conditions before release or record the unverified regime as an open premise in the release sign-off.

↩︎ A policy whose actions choose its next observations drives the endogenous covariate shift established in Mechanics of Endogenous Covariate Shift.

Closing this gap requires matching the data-collection and evaluation campaign to the restricted operating envelope. Expert demonstrations must span the admitted transport velocities, insertion forces, and thermal regimes, preserving complete site metadata in the provenance record. Once trained, the candidate proposer must undergo closed-loop trials on the physical cell to confirm that it completes the packaging task under those conditions, including during interventions by the permission path. If the cell cannot maintain acceptable task performance within its physical bounds, engineers must revise the task specification, the proposer architecture, or the mechanical plant before re-evaluating the modified system. Calculating a speed ceiling tests the transport stopping budget under its stated premises; qualifying those premises and demonstrating closed-loop task performance are both required for a viable packaging cell.

↩︎ Why proposer evidence must come from the closed loop the machine will actually run is established in Closed-Loop Dynamics.

The third law places these proposed actions beneath an architectural authority split that the proposers cannot bypass, delay, or corrupt. The permission path prevents unverified neural proposals from reaching physical actuators directly. In this packaging cell, an independent kinematic monitor runs at 1 kHz, reading the carriage’s optical encoders and a rangefinder aimed at the gate, and admits a carriage motion command only when the measured clearance supports the qualified stopping distance. This monitor is the admission stage of the camera path in notebook 18.1: the camera reports that a closure has begun, and the monitor admits the stop. Its work is therefore part of the 36 ms processing allocation, and a 1 kHz tick bounds only how long a command waits for the next check, not the transfer, inference, and scheduling that come before it.

While the software kinematic check confines normal operation to the derated velocity mode, it does not carry the cell’s formal protection claims. In an industrial deployment, harm-prevention claims rest on hardware-certified drive safety functions codified under IEC 61800-5-2 (International Electrotechnical Commission 2016) and mapped for warehouse automation in The permission path above its certified floor. The restricted transport mode maps to safely limited speed (SLS) on the carriage drive, an independent hardware function that continuously monitors motor velocity and triggers an emergency stop if the limit is exceeded. The emergency response executes as a ramp-monitored safe stop 1 (SS1) terminating in safe torque off (STO), with safe brake control (SBC) engaging mechanical holding brakes at rest. Each safety function requires a validated timing budget and an integrity metric. Under ISO 13849-1 (International Organization for Standardization 2023), safety functions are classified by performance level (PL), which bounds their average probability of dangerous failure per hour according to the cell’s risk assessment. The camera-triggered stop budgeted in notebook 18.1 is the application-level path announced before it, not a protective safety function. A safety-rated optical gate sensor feeding the drive’s SS1 input directly replaces the frame-exposure and neural-inference allocations with its certified hardware response time, while command delivery and brake onset still contribute to \(\PrebrakeDelay\) in the physical stopping model.

International Electrotechnical Commission. 2016. IEC 61800-5-2:2016: Adjustable Speed Electrical Power Drive Systems, Part 5-2: Safety Requirements, Functional. International Electrotechnical Commission.
International Organization for Standardization. 2023. ISO 13849-1:2023: Safety of Machinery, Safety-Related Parts of Control Systems, Part 1: General Principles for Design. International Organization for Standardization.

Physical safety limits must govern the insertion tool and actuator drives just as strictly as carriage motion. Setting a mechanical force limiter to 220 N is a nominal design choice rather than proof that every parcel resists crushing. Physical tests must verify that peak transmitted forces, including dynamic overshoot during contact impact, stay below the yield strength of the weakest admitted carton and tab geometry. Similarly, setting a winding thermistor trip threshold at 105 °C and a stall detection timeout of 400 ms provides basic supervisory cutoffs rather than a certified thermal envelope. Safe continuous operation depends on drive current profiles, winding resistance, heat capacity, cooling airflow, and sensor thermal lag. Because internal winding temperatures can surge before external housing sensors register the heat, as modeled in Thermal capacity and insulation breakdown thresholds, engineers cannot validate a sustained duty cycle from trip thresholds alone without measuring the thermal response under load.

High-rate deceleration also transfers mechanical kinetic energy back into the electrical drive system. During regenerative braking, the decelerating motor returns power to the inverter DC bus. A dynamic brake chopper and power resistor must absorb this energy before bus voltage exceeds the inverter trip threshold of 60 V, following the bus energy balance in Regenerative overvoltage surges and dynamic brake choppers. The energy sink must handle repeated stops across a full shift rather than an isolated emergency event. Furthermore, regenerative braking requires an energized inverter stage throughout deceleration, whereas the carriage’s electromechanical track brake relies on its own spring-actuated mechanism. Logic power hold-up alone supplies neither regenerative braking torque nor friction-brake clamping force. A thermal fault or electrical shutdown that cuts drive power disables regenerative braking, leaving deceleration entirely to the mechanical brake. The notebook’s 15 m/s² bound therefore holds after a power cut only if the spring-applied track brake is itself qualified to it. Otherwise the power-cut case has a lower deceleration, a longer stop, and a lower speed ceiling of its own. System designers must ensure that safety-directed power cuts preserve a qualified deceleration and holding response across the declared operating states and fault conditions.

The fourth law synthesizes these engineering decisions into a release argument. The cell’s release manifest must validate rangefinder coverage, gate clearance margins, braking deceleration, and sustained task throughput as one connected system. Equally important, the manifest explicitly records any claims that physical evidence cannot support. To check whether the tabs have latched, the engineering team evaluates a vision model that inspects surface reflections for container tab engagement. Under industrial overhead lighting, glare from tape and crinkled plastic packaging produces bright reflections that mimic latch defects while hiding genuine tab misalignments. Because the cell lacks an independent physical measurement, such as a tactile switch or a laser profile scanner, that separates engaged tabs from misaligned flaps under glare, the sign-off claim lacks a qualified detector. Recording this limitation as detectorless and unsupported in the residual-claims register (section 18.3.3) is not a documentation failure but an accurate statement of what the physical evidence justifies.

The stopping analysis therefore supports a candidate restricted mode for carriage transit at 0.50 m/s, provided that the 64 ms end-to-end latency, 15 m/s² braking deceleration, 60 mm physical clearance, and uncertainty reserve are validated together on the physical cell. Insertion forces and thermal duty cycles remain restricted until physical testing confirms material crush limits, sensor thermal lag, and independent fault paths. Autonomous package sign-off is refused because glare hides tab engagement from the vision system and the cell lacks an independent verification sensor. A qualified tactile or laser engagement sensor could support autonomous sign-off; mandatory downstream manual inspection instead supports a commercial deployment that makes no autonomous sign-off claim. An analytical throughput model cannot substitute for that physical evidence. Each law’s answer constrains the next. The stopping mechanics fix a transport envelope, the envelope fixes the regime that data collection and evaluation must cover, the runtime monitors enforce that envelope, and the release argument restricts the cell wherever a claim lacks support.

The same audit extends beyond the three machine classes of the packaging cell into fluid and chemical process systems, where the irreversible consequence may be reagent already delivered or heat already transferred rather than damage from a moving mass. Consider a learned proposer that sets the opening of a dosing valve feeding a reaction tank, with a high-level trip that commands the valve shut before the tank overfills. The first law asks what reagent has already been committed. If the shutoff command arrives after a delay \(\PrebrakeDelay\) at a volumetric flow rate \(\VolumetricFlowRate\), fluid continues to discharge. The discharge adds roughly \(\VolumetricFlowRate\PrebrakeDelay\) to the volume delivered before the valve even begins to move, the fluidic analog of the delay travel \(\SpeedAtDetection\PrebrakeDelay\) in the stopping distance. The valve’s closing stroke then adds the fluidic analog of braking distance, and both must fit within the headroom above the trip level, as the clearance did for the carriage. Stored thermodynamic and hydraulic energy also complicates the fallback. A pressurized accumulator, a cryogenic transfer line, or a heated pressure vessel retains stored energy after electronic control power is cut. As with a balancing biped, whose fall begins the moment its torque is removed, de-energizing the controller does not by itself produce a safe state. The design must therefore name a qualified physical sink, such as a spring-loaded pressure relief bypass, to dissipate that energy. The remaining laws apply in the same way. Policy evaluation must sample the throttled flow regimes that the safety limits enforce, the permission path must guarantee valve closure within the allowable volume tolerance, and sign-off must rest on measured valve slew rates and sensor response times. In every case the machine’s operating claims hold only as far as its physical evidence reaches, and a physical state that stays unobserved until the execution deadline marks where that evidence ends.

Checkpoint 18.1: Transferring the four-law audit

The packaging cell demonstrates how the four-law audit organizes system design for an entirely novel machine, anchoring every operational claim to physical bounds and empirical measurements.

18.3 The Epistemic Frontier

A packaging cell’s camera can confirm an arm’s position and approve a transport motion without verifying that a hidden mechanical tab has engaged. Similarly, a warehouse robot’s lidar and cameras can clear a navigation path across an aisle without determining whether the concrete floor provides enough traction to brake. These gaps are epistemic limits: boundaries on what the available sensors and analytical arguments can justify. Additional training can improve a policy’s action proposals, but machine learning cannot manufacture an unmeasured physical state. Systems engineers must therefore determine whether justifying the next operating claim requires broader training experience or a physical measurement absent from the existing sensor suite.

Consider an aisle where a pallet jack with a leaking hydraulic line has just crossed, leaving a thin, clear film of fluid on polished concrete. The mobile manipulator’s stopping budget assumes the floor supplies enough surface friction for credible deceleration, which dry industrial concrete reliably does. Under an oil film, however, available traction collapses, yet neither standard navigation cameras nor time-of-flight lidars can tell the clear fluid apart from dry concrete. In this failure mode the safety architecture runs exactly as specified. The software permission path meets every deadline, chunk leases expire on schedule, and the drive controller applies braking torque right on time. Even so, the robot slides past its stop line and collides at the rack end. The failure comes not from software latency or actuator lag, but from an unmonitored premise, the local wheel-floor friction coefficient before braking, and the stopping budget prices that premise.

The claim adjudication in Matching the argument to the machine already encountered this premise: claim C3 (braking traction) was refused on this book’s own evidence and deferred to this chapter as an unresolved premise. The credible deceleration \(\CredibleBrakingDeceleration\) that The Physical Body credited to the brakes was always conditional on the floor. Made explicit, that condition says the deceleration a machine actually has available is the smaller of what its brakes can deliver and what tire-floor traction can transmit:1 \[\AvailableDeceleration = \min(\CredibleBrakingDeceleration, \CoulombFrictionCoefficient \Gravity). \tag{1}\] The friction term is the traction ceiling of The Physical Body (figure and Electrical time constants, back-EMF, and current clamping), \(\CoulombFrictionCoefficient \DrivenWheelLoadFraction \Gravity\), taken with all of the weight on the driven wheels (\(\DrivenWheelLoadFraction = 1\)). That is the case that sets the friction floor, the same optimistic all-weight screen that claim C3 was adjudicated against. At the drive wheels’ rated load share the floor is higher, as that notebook computes, so every friction margin below is the optimistic one. On an oily surface, interface friction rather than motor torque becomes the binding constraint.

1 Amontons-Coulomb Friction: The classical laws of sliding friction make the friction force proportional to the normal load and independent of the apparent contact area, which is what lets a single coefficient \(\CoulombFrictionCoefficient\) describe a contact. Bowden and Tabor (1950) traced the law to the small true area of contact, whose shear strength sets \(\CoulombFrictionCoefficient\), and showed that a lubricant film only molecules thick can lower it several-fold. The coefficient therefore belongs to the pair of surfaces and whatever lies between them, not to the floor alone, and the braking premise needs a measurement at the tire-floor interface that no camera viewing the floor supplies.

Bowden, Frank Philip, and David Tabor. 1950. The Friction and Lubrication of Solids. International Series of Monographs on Physics. Clarendon Press.

Napkin Math 18.2: The friction floor
Problem: A clear hydraulic film lies across the aisle. Does the known-component stop from the candidate 1.3 m/s aisle speed still fit the rack end’s 1.10 m clear distance?

Variables:

  • Credible deceleration \(\CredibleBrakingDeceleration\) = 2 m/s² (Illustrative, Kinetic Momentum) and \(\Gravity\) = 9.81 m/s² (Defined standard-gravity constant)
  • Floor friction \(\CoulombFrictionCoefficient\) = 0.60 on dry industrial concrete and 0.05 under the film (both Illustrative; see the Reader Guide)
  • Aisle speed \(\SpeedAtDetection\) = 1.3 m/s (Chosen) and clear distance \(\ClearDistance\) = 1.10 m (Illustrative)
  • Known-component budget, the seven rows of The warehouse mobile manipulator's stopping budget, which leave out the dispatch-to-admission interval \(\DispatchAdmissionInterval\) that is allocated but not yet measured: pre-brake delay \(\PrebrakeDelay\) = 133.6 ms, jerk bound \(\JerkLimit\) = 7.11 m/s³, and fixed overheads of 190 mm (all Derived)

Math:

  1. Friction floor (equation 1): The brake binds only while \(\CoulombFrictionCoefficient \ge \CredibleBrakingDeceleration/\Gravity\) = 2 m/s² \(/\) 9.81 m/s² = 0.204. Dry concrete clears it; the film does not, so \(\AvailableDeceleration = \CoulombFrictionCoefficient \Gravity\) = 0.05 \(\times\) 9.81 m/s² = 0.49 m/s².
  2. Dry-floor budget: Delay travel \(\SpeedAtDetection\PrebrakeDelay\) \(+\) braking \(\SpeedAtDetection^2/2\AvailableDeceleration\) \(+\) jerk-ramp travel \(\SpeedAtDetection \AvailableDeceleration/2\JerkLimit\) (the S-curve term of row 6, valid in the trapezoidal regime because \(\SpeedAtDetection > \AvailableDeceleration^2/\JerkLimit\)) \(+\) overheads = 173.7 mm \(+\) 422.5 mm \(+\) 182.8 mm \(+\) 190 mm = 969.0 mm.
  3. Film budget: The same terms at \(\AvailableDeceleration\) = 0.49 m/s² give 173.7 mm \(+\) 1,723.3 mm \(+\) 44.8 mm \(+\) 190 mm = 2.13 m.

Result: On the film the stop grows from 969.0 mm to 2.13 m, far past the 1.10 m clear distance, before the dispatch-to-admission interval is even added.

Systems insight: Below \(\CoulombFrictionCoefficient\) = 0.204 the floor, not the brake or the timing, sets the deceleration, and the braking term grows as \(1/\CoulombFrictionCoefficient\). Faster admission can recover only the 173.7 mm of delay travel, so no improvement in the permission path restores the dry-floor mode on the film. Only a restriction or a measurement at the tire-floor interface can keep \(\StoppingDistance \le \ClearDistance\).

Below \(\CoulombFrictionCoefficient\) = 0.204, the floor, not the brake, sets the deceleration.

The calculation omits the dispatch-to-admission interval, which the discrete placement of The board-link budget and the dispatch-to-admission interval allocates but no loaded measurement yet confirms. That interval adds travel and reduces every derived speed ceiling, yet even the known-component budget already excludes the film. Moreover, the friction premise did not appear only when fluid leaked onto the concrete. The candidate normal operating mode of Sizing the composite stopping budget always assumed that unmeasured friction floor across every aisle, dry or wet. Software timing and physical traction are independent requirements, since bounding scheduling delays does not guarantee floor grip and confirming floor grip does not remove scheduling delays. On the oil film, the drive electronics continue to regulate motor current and commanded torque within milliseconds, yet that torque slows the base only through tire-floor contact. Neither a faster enforcer nor another layer of neural inference therefore replaces the interface measurement or the speed restriction the notebook calls for.

↩︎ Current regulation within milliseconds through electrical time constants and current clamping is established in Electrical time constants, back-EMF, and current clamping.

The manipulator arm’s contact limit at the door latch has the same structure. Safety depends on a latch that holds under any force below the arm’s contact ceiling. Mechanical wear, material fatigue, or corrosion can silently lower the latch’s yield force below that ceiling, even while joint torque sensors report readings identical to those against a sound strike plate. A wrist force-torque sensor whose zero-point calibration has drifted creates the same hazard for every contact threshold that depends on its signal (section 18.3.4.2).

In each case, a machine can leave its safe operating envelope while internal telemetry stays completely nominal. Successful test runs under nominal conditions provide no safety guarantee once an underlying physical premise fails. A safety case must explicitly list each unobserved premise, restrict operating authority while that premise remains unverified, and state the physical evidence required to close it. For every open entry, the engineer must determine what the machine can physically measure, what actuator limits can still prevent, and which operating claims must remain refused.

18.3.1 What the release claim rests on

Extensive testing of one link in a safety case cannot compensate for an unmeasured premise in another: a thousand hours of fault-free perception trials will not stop a base whose tires lose traction on an oily floor.2 A mobile manipulator’s release claim forms a chain of dependent arguments tied to its operating hazards and chosen mitigations. The complete safety case must establish the operational design domain, measured actuator performance, stopping bounds, component interface contracts, and independent permission filtering for learned proposals. Hazards managed by runtime detection require verified detector coverage and bounded monitoring latency, whereas hazards managed through passive compliance or operating restrictions require physical evidence that those safeguards hold across declared payloads, component wear, and site conditions.

2 Popperian Falsification: The asymmetry holds for universal hypotheses (Popper 1959). No finite run of safe stops proves that every aisle floor supplies the friction floor, and one measured film below it refutes that premise. A rate claim, such as a hazard rate per hour, is neither refuted by one incident nor proved by many incident-free hours, so it is bounded statistically and revised as exposure accumulates. This chapter therefore holds deterministic premises in register entries and rate claims in confidence bounds.

Popper, Karl R. 1959. The Logic of Scientific Discovery. Hutchinson.

Each premise in a safety case carries one of five statuses: established by measurement or derivation, assumed, decided by accountable judgment, unsupported, or outside the threat model. (The Reader Guide lists the same five with examples; the audits of this chapter and its problems use these exact labels.) The envelope trichotomy (definition 16.3) classifies an actively monitored runtime condition as known-true, known-false, or unknown. That trichotomy presupposes a monitor. “Unknown” marks a monitored condition whose evidence has lapsed, and the permission path answers it at once with a validated stop or hold until fresh evidence returns. An unobserved premise has no runtime monitor whose report can trigger that transition; its missing evidence must instead be recorded in the release argument. Telemetry stays nominal while the premise fails, and holding the machine until a report arrives would hold it forever, because no report will come. Such premises require standing restrictions decided before operation; a fallback cannot be triggered by evidence the machine never receives. Release certification must therefore either validate and maintain these premises through external physical evidence or restrict operating authority accordingly. For the mobile base, stopping-distance guarantees rest on an assumed wheel-floor friction coefficient that cannot be observed before braking. For the manipulator arm, contact safety assumes a minimum yield strength for the door latch and the objects it handles, a threshold set by engineering judgment rather than real-time measurement. For the wrist sensor, force-limiting safety functions assume zero-point calibration stability, a condition internal telemetry cannot verify without an unloaded physical tare. Recording these premises explicitly does not weaken a release case; it marks the exact boundary where system guarantees hold and beyond which operating authority must be withdrawn.

Separating physical measurement from accountable judgment is essential to systems engineering. Testing and statistical analysis can estimate physical exposure, quantify sensor noise, and bound parameter uncertainty, but test data cannot choose an acceptable residual risk or decide which people in a facility must bear that risk. Those choices are accountable engineering decisions that must be recorded as explicit judgments rather than presented as empirical facts. Similarly, the verification campaign’s threat model bounds where the safety record holds. Field trials across normal fleet workflows and random component faults do not by themselves support claims about deliberate attacks outside the tested conditions. A test campaign showing that a vision pipeline tolerates lens flare, sensor noise, and partial occlusions provides no evidence that it resists adversarial optical projections, tampered policy weights, or deliberate denial-of-service flooding on the internal communication bus.

↩︎ The fault list that bounds what verification tests, including transduction-layer attacks on sensors and on the proposer, is established in Deriving the Fault List.

18.3.2 Conditional shield credit and common-cause bounds

Treating an independent permission path as a substitute for a missing floor-friction measurement asks the safety shield to stop a hazard it cannot sense. Even for hazards within its view, a shield reduces the failure rate only in proportion to its own tested coverage. Pricing what the permission path actually contributes to a hazard-rate claim separates the hazards a shield can attenuate from the register entries it can never close. The Minimal Intervention Principle and Optimization Filters established deterministic guarantees for each command a safety shield checks, but turning those per-command guarantees into a system-level hazard rate requires statistical accounting. Because a shield acts as a conditional filter, the resulting hazard rate depends on how often the policy proposes hazardous actions, how reliably the shield intercepts them in those exact states, and how often failures occur outside the shield’s sensing domain. Notebook 18.3 prices these terms in an illustrative deployment.

Consider an illustrative system target of 1 × 10⁻⁹ \(\text{h}^{-1}\). This number comes from civil aviation certification, where catastrophic failure conditions must be shown to be “extremely improbable,” on the order of \(10^{-9}\) per flight hour3 (Federal Aviation Administration 1988). While no industrial robotics standard requires this threshold, examining it shows the statistical burden a borrowed safety target imposes. As the exposure wall established, physical operating hours alone cannot certify failure rates of this scale.4 The practical engineering question is what safety margin a runtime shield genuinely contributes against that target, and what supporting evidence its own operation requires.

3 Extremely Improbable Band: The advisory circular offers its probability bands as aids to engineering judgment, expressed per flight hour of a mean-duration flight and applied to each failure condition separately, and it grounds such criteria in historical accident data (Federal Aviation Administration 1988). The \(10^{-9}\) figure is therefore one catastrophic condition’s share of a certified design’s risk, not a rate for a whole aircraft, let alone for another kind of machine. Applying it elsewhere without the allocation argument that set its share leaves the number without its basis.

Federal Aviation Administration. 1988. System Design and Analysis. AC 25.1309-1A. U.S. Department of Transportation, Federal Aviation Administration.

4 Pooling Evidence Across Updates: An updated model may alter the proposal distribution. Earlier data can be pooled only with a justified configuration-equivalence and exposure argument; an update does not automatically erase every observation. The exposure wall’s constant-rate model also requires a defensible treatment of shared fleet conditions and correlated faults.

↩︎ The exposure wall, the event-free operating hours needed to bound a rare hazard rate, is established in Verification Ceilings and the Exposure Wall.

Sha, Lui. 2001. “Using Simplicity to Control Complexity.” IEEE Software 18 (4): 20–28.

A safety shield argument is inherently conditional. Observed incident rates across an operating fleet already include the protective interventions of runtime filters and human supervisors, so multiplying an observed incident rate by a shield’s interception factor double-counts that protection. For a defined hazard class \(\HazardClassIndex\), let \(\HazardousProposalRate\) denote the arrival rate of unshielded, hazardous action proposals emitted by the policy before the permission layer. Let \(\ShieldInterceptionCoverage = \Pr(\text{intercept} \mid \text{hazardous proposal}, \HazardClassIndex)\) denote the verified interception coverage for that hazard class. Including an independently bounded hardware fault and common-cause arrival rate \(\HardwareCommonCauseRate\), the conditional hazard rate obeys equation 2: \[\SystemHazardRate \le \HazardousProposalRate(1-\ShieldInterceptionCoverage)+\HardwareCommonCauseRate \tag{2}\] Here, \(\SystemHazardRate\), \(\HazardousProposalRate\), and \(\HardwareCommonCauseRate\) are rates per operating hour, while \(\ShieldInterceptionCoverage\) is a unitless conditional probability. The limiting cases make the shield’s role plain. With perfect detection and interception (\(\ShieldInterceptionCoverage = 1\)), the upper bound reduces to the hardware and common-cause term \(\HardwareCommonCauseRate\), a residual no coverage removes. Conversely, if the shield has zero coverage (\(\ShieldInterceptionCoverage = 0\)), the raw proposal hazard rate passes straight to the actuators. This relation turns the runtime assurance architecture, which pairs an unverified primary controller with a verified safety filter (Sha 2001; Hsu et al. 2024), into a checkable hazard-rate claim.

↩︎ The Simplex architecture, which pairs a capable unverified proposer with a verified protective controller, is established in The Per-Tick Gatekeeper.

Using equation 2 requires four premises. The first is a verified counterfactual oracle: an evaluation harness that determines whether an unshielded action proposal would have caused a safety violation. Without this oracle or another justified way to identify or conservatively bound hazardous proposals, the raw proposal error rate \(\HazardousProposalRate\) has no supporting evidence. The remaining requirements are timely hazard detection, adequate braking authority, and a careful accounting of correlated failures. Designers frequently overlook this final common-cause term. Release claim C5 required physical testing rather than a theoretical independence assumption because separate implementations of the same specification fail together far more often than statistical independence predicts (Contextual assumptions and the weakest link). The product \(\HazardousProposalRate(1-\ShieldInterceptionCoverage)\) uses interception coverage conditional on hazardous proposals; substituting coverage measured on unrelated states erases that conditioning. An occluded camera frame that causes the neural policy to propose a bad trajectory can corrupt the safety shield’s state estimator at the same moment. That shared failure does not merely add an independent background term to \(\HardwareCommonCauseRate\); it lowers \(\ShieldInterceptionCoverage\) in the same states where policy errors happen. Interception coverage \(\ShieldInterceptionCoverage\) must therefore be measured across the actual states where the policy fails rather than on an idealized set of synthetic test cases. The equation gives no credit for hazards the shield cannot sense, such as oil films or fatigue-weakened latches, and no credit for the shield at all until each of the four premises is supported. Until then the release must restrict operation instead.

Bounding the system hazard rate requires bounding the raw proposal rate \(\HazardousProposalRate\) from an adjudicated execution log before crediting shield attenuation. Under a stationary Poisson arrival model with zero observed hazardous proposals, this upper bound follows the rule of three (Hanley and Lippman-Hand 1983) from equation 7.4, with proposal operating hours in place of discrete trials. For an adjudicated log spanning \(\Time\) operating hours at significance level \(\HypothesisTestSignificanceLevel\), the one-sided upper confidence bound is \(-\ln\HypothesisTestSignificanceLevel/\Time\), or roughly \(3/\Time\) at 95 percent confidence. When the interception coverage \(\ShieldInterceptionCoverage\) is also estimated empirically from zero-miss seeded trials rather than given as a known constant, the product \(\HazardousProposalRate(1-\ShieldInterceptionCoverage)\) rests on two separate confidence bounds that can each fail. Guaranteeing that both bounds hold simultaneously at overall significance \(\HypothesisTestSignificanceLevel\) requires splitting the error budget across the continuous proposal-log test and the discrete coverage-trial test via the union bound, testing each factor at significance \(\HypothesisTestSignificanceLevel/2\):

Hanley, James A., and Abby Lippman-Hand. 1983. “If Nothing Goes Wrong, Is Everything All Right? Interpreting Zero Numerators.” JAMA 249 (13): 1743–45.

Napkin Math 18.3: The coverage bill
Problem: In an illustrative scenario, a fleet keeps a fully adjudicated proposal log for one release cycle, judged by a validated counterfactual oracle, and the log records no hazardous proposal. If a named shield’s interception coverage for that hazard class were independently established, would the modeled residual of equation 2 meet the system target, and what evidence would it take to show that it does?

Variables:

  • System target \(\TargetHazardRate\) = 1 × 10⁻⁹ \(\text{h}^{-1}\) (Illustrative)
  • Proposal log \(\Time\) = 100 units \(\times\) 90 days \(\times\) 24 h = 2.16 × 10⁵ proposal-hours with zero hazardous proposals (Illustrative)
  • Class-specific coverage \(\ShieldInterceptionCoverage\) = 0.9999 and \(\HardwareCommonCauseRate\) = 0 (Illustrative; the hardware and common-cause term is set to zero for this calculation)
  • Confidence 95 percent, significance \(\HypothesisTestSignificanceLevel\) = 0.05 (Chosen)

Math:

  1. Proposal bound (rule of three with hours in place of trials): \(-\ln\HypothesisTestSignificanceLevel / \Time\) = 2.996 \(/\) 2.16 × 10⁵ h = 1.387 × 10⁻⁵ \(\text{h}^{-1}\).
  2. Residual: 1.387 × 10⁻⁵ \(\times\) 1 × 10⁻⁴ \(+\) 0 = 1.387 × 10⁻⁹ \(\text{h}^{-1}\), above the target. Meeting it at this bound needs \(\ShieldInterceptionCoverage \ge 1 -\) 1 × 10⁻⁹ \(/\) 1.387 × 10⁻⁵ = 0.9999279.
  3. Proposal evidence at the assumed coverage: The bound must fall to 1 × 10⁻⁹ \(/\) 1 × 10⁻⁴ = 1 × 10⁻⁵ \(\text{h}^{-1}\), which takes 2.996 \(/\) 1 × 10⁻⁵ = 3.00 × 10⁵ proposal-hours, or 124.8 days for the fleet, 34.8 days beyond the cycle.
  4. Coverage evidence: Bounding \(\ShieldInterceptionCoverage\) itself takes \(\ln\) 0.05 \(/ \ln\) 0.9999 = 29,956 seeded hazardous proposals of that class with no miss, drawn from the states in which the policy errs.
  5. Joint confidence: The probability that at least one of two bounds fails is at most the sum of their failure probabilities, so a joint 95 percent statement needs each bound at 97.5 percent (the Bonferroni split). The proposal bound becomes 3.689 \(/\) 2.16 × 10⁵ h = 1.708 × 10⁻⁵ \(\text{h}^{-1}\), the coverage campaign 36,887 trials, and the residual 1.708 × 10⁻⁹ \(\text{h}^{-1}\).

Result: The modeled residual misses the target even with the common-cause term set to zero. Supporting the claim takes both a proposal log longer than the release cycle and tens of thousands of seeded interceptions, and the confidence of the combined claim must be budgeted across them.

Systems insight: A shield changes the system claim only for hazard classes it can detect and intercept in time. Proposal frequency, conditional interception coverage, hardware faults, and common-cause failures need separate evidence. Oil or material failure outside the shield’s sensing coverage stays in the residual-claims register.

The notebook prices what evidence the exposure model and the safety filter would need; it supplies none for either. Formal verification carries the same scoping requirement, since any mathematical proof applies only to the declared kinematic model, sensor observations, disturbance bounds, and timing guarantees. Whenever a physical premise lacks empirical proof, the release argument must substitute an operating restriction assigned to an accountable owner.

↩︎ The scoping limits of formal certificates to their declared plant, observations, and disturbances are established in Scope of a formal verification result.

18.3.3 A residual-claims register

Refusing claim C3 in the safety case did not remove the floor-friction premise behind it. Without a durable record tying that open premise to an operating restriction, a later model update could restore normal aisle speed while the floor remains unmeasured. The residual-claims register is the sixteenth and closing record in the systems record chain. Taking in the upstream release manifest and approval record, the register binds every unresolved physical premise directly to an operating restriction.

↪ The sixteen-record chain, which the residual-claims register closes, is developed in The record register.

Each register entry names four required elements: the missing physical observable, the active containment mechanism or operating restriction, the accountable owner, and a predeclared closure test. Together they give principle 4 direct operational force. Unlike a software defect log or issue tracker, the register is a machine-readable deployment record with a fixed schema. Its parameters set runtime enforcer bounds, and its standing conditions bind facility operations. Operating authority stays derated until test evidence passes the predeclared closure test. Writing a warning note in documentation, or deferring an open premise to a future release without lowering speed or narrowing the operational design domain, provides zero physical protection.

↪ Byte layout: Wire formats, data fields, and structural invariants for register entries are documented in Residual-claims register.

Not every open premise is physical. Entries that the approval record of The Approval Record cannot close before release, such as a human role whose feasibility is still UNKNOWN, a decision waiting for an owner, or a group of people whose detection has not yet been measured, enter the register in the same form. Each carries the restriction that holds meanwhile, such as a lower speed wherever the unmeasured group works, an owner, and the test that would close it.

Engineers must state each missing observable in concrete physical quantities rather than modeling jargon. Calling a failure an “out-of-distribution latent state” does not say what physical sensor must measure it or when an enforcer must intervene. Naming an unmeasured friction coefficient or latch yield force, by contrast, makes the engineering choices concrete. The team can weigh adding a sensor, adding a passive mechanical limit, or narrowing the operational design domain, and everyone can see what test evidence is needed to retire the restriction.

The undetected oil film forms the first register entry for the mobile manipulator. Its premise is that every aisle floor supplies a friction coefficient of at least \(\CoulombFrictionCoefficient \ge\) 0.204, the floor needed for credible deceleration. The missing observable is the local friction coefficient under a clear fluid film, which neither onboard cameras nor lidars can tell apart from clean concrete. Because a stop sized for dry concrete overruns the available clearance on an oily floor, the normal speed limit is justified only on surfaces where friction is verified or actively controlled.

An open premise requires an immediate operational response. For warehouse aisles, release sign-off can authorize a derated speed of 1 m/s on designated routes maintained under a facility inspection procedure. In this restricted-service mode, scheduled floor scrubbing, spill isolation, and controlled access must maintain a friction coefficient of at least \(\CoulombFrictionCoefficient \ge\) 0.12. That friction level gives an available deceleration of 1.18 m/s² and a known-component speed ceiling of 1.23 m/s. This is the inspected-floor ceiling that the release envelope carries (Operating envelope of the warehouse mobile manipulator), drawn as the dashed envelope in the stopping distance analysis of Safety Enforcement (figure). The restricted mode sits below that ceiling rather than at it, because the ceiling, like the budget it comes from, still leaves out \(\DispatchAdmissionInterval\) and any reserve for it. This inspected level is an illustrative baseline chosen below dry concrete to tolerate light surface contamination. A production deployment must set this value from friction surveys across the designated routes and show how spill prevention or bounded cleanup maintains it between inspections. In the illustrative project records, restricted-mode operation remains provisional until the full timing budget, including dispatch-to-admission latency and the required reserves, is verified under load. Routes outside the facility inspection program are excluded from automated navigation.

Restricted operation requires an accountable owner who can keep the underlying physical premise true. Facility operations owns this entry through its maintenance and spill-response procedures along pallet-jack routes, setting inspection intervals and specifying the site conditions that pause automated traffic. While the signed speed ceiling in the onboard permission path enforces the motion limit, a software speed setting alone cannot keep the floor dry.

Every register entry must also state a closure test before data collection begins. To detect surface films online, the closure test must qualify an independent physical sensor, since standard visual cameras and lidars cannot resolve clear fluids on concrete. Alternatively, higher-traction floor coatings or physical barriers separating leaky equipment could support a revised claim through facility evidence. One candidate closure campaign evaluates an optical film detector, requiring a miss rate of at most 0.1 percent demonstrated across 2,995 zero-failure trials on seeded fluid films, the trial count required by the zero-failure sizing of Sample size requirements per empirical claim. Until the sensor passes this test, the lower speed limit stays in force. Smaller test campaigns do not provide enough statistical confidence. Zero misses across 1,000 independent trials bounds the miss probability only at 0.002991 at 95 percent confidence. In addition, the detector must see far enough ahead that sensing latency, permission processing, and braking finish before the wheels reach the fluid. Forward range, inference latency, false-alarm rate, and post-detection friction assumptions must all be declared in advance. Reactive wheel-slip reflexes can limit further acceleration once the wheels spin, but they cannot guarantee stopping distance before the tires hit the film.

While the target miss rate of 0.1 percent is chosen illustratively here, a production deployment must derive it from the site’s hazard budget. The detector works inside the conditional shield relation of equation 2, with fluid film encounters in place of hazardous action proposals. If films appear along transit routes at an arrival rate \(\FilmEncounterRate\) and facility engineering allocates a hazard budget \(\AllocatedHazardRate\) to this failure mode, the detector must satisfy \(\FilmEncounterRate\,\DetectorMissProbability \le \AllocatedHazardRate\). Choosing the risk allocation \(\AllocatedHazardRate\) is an accountable engineering judgment, whereas estimating the encounter rate \(\FilmEncounterRate\) comes from maintenance and spill logs.

Aisle navigation adds a second register entry: moving people at blind corners. Stopping-distance budgets are sized against stationary obstacles, so a person who keeps walking toward the mobile base during the stop consumes clearance distance that the budget never set aside. As shown in Sizing the composite stopping budget, accounting for a person walking toward the robot throughout the stop caps the permissible base speed at the rack corner far below normal transit speeds. The safety premise behind normal aisle speed assumes that no person walks out of the occluded cross aisle during an emergency stop, leaving a person’s presence in the hidden cross aisle as an unobserved state. Restricted service therefore requires physical barriers or access controls that block human cross-traffic during automated runs; painted floor lines do not satisfy this condition. Facility operations owns this restriction. A proposed blind-corner presence sensor cannot earn credit in the safety case until its field of view, latency, and miss rate pass qualification under the same zero-failure statistical standard applied to the film detector.

↩︎ Clearance eroded by a person approaching from an occluded cross aisle at walking speed is established in Forward State Propagation and Occlusion Decay.

The automated picking station adds a third entry, covering what the arm grasps. Its premise is that the object the gripper picks up, and then hands to a coworker through the accept_item interface, is the object the task names. As shown in Intent Grounding Failure Modes and Their Checks, semantic grounding can fail silently. When a thin-walled red glass jar sits alone where the red ceramic mug was expected, the grounding model mistakes the jar for the mug and still satisfies every geometric check in the planner and permission layers. The missing observables are object identity and crush strength, which neither wrist cameras nor visual foundation models can verify reliably before contact.

↩︎ The accept_item handover entry, which grants no motion authority and caps the arm’s approach to the coworker’s hand, is established in Capability matrices and the intervention-enforcement split.

Containing this hazard takes a two-part operating restriction. First, facility operations restricts conveyor loading to verified items from a declared stock-keeping unit (SKU) list that excludes fragile look-alikes. Second, the pick station admits a grasp only when optical singulation confirms a single object in the pick window; multiple objects abort the automated intent and route the tote to manual picking. Singulation removes geometric confusion among permitted items, but it does not measure what an object is made of; an off-list glass jar arriving alone is kept out only by upstream loading controls. Lowering the grip-force limit enough to protect fragile items would require measured crush limits for every item in inventory, which is itself an unverified physical assumption. Facility operations and the application engineering team co-own the item catalog and singulation rules. The closure test requires qualifying an automated check at the station, such as a radio-frequency identification (RFID) reader or a combined scale-and-barcode check, with a false-accept rate of at most 0.1 percent at 95 percent confidence across 2,995 failure-free trials with seeded look-alikes. Table 2 compares these three entries side by side, listing the four required components for each open premise.

The register also holds a fourth entry, a timing premise, which the table leaves out because its hazard is the same overrun as the first row’s and only its missing evidence differs. Measured capture-to-dispatch latency excludes the downstream dispatch-to-admission interval. The placement allocation budgets that interval at 63.3 ms out of the 100.8 ms timing allowance at the aisle speed, leaving 37.5 ms of reserve. Stated in the register’s four elements, the missing observable is the loaded worst-case dispatch-to-admission interval, which steady-state inference benchmarks do not bound. Because that interval remains unverified, the runtime systems team owns an active restriction that holds the aisle speed as an uncertified candidate rather than an authorized mode. The closure test is supported timing and response analysis under declared workload and resource bounds, challenged by loaded and adversarial measurements of both latency intervals. Until that test shows that the interval fits its allocation with positive reserve and meets the lease duration and renewal requirements of the execution contracts, the restriction stays in force. The stipulated service branch assumes that timing evidence for its derated mode; in the baseline records the entry remains open.

Maintaining the restrictions

Writing an open assumption into a machine-readable register file does not turn it into a measured physical state. If the signed speed limits and site procedures are not wired into the release gate and daily floor work, the restriction exists only on paper. Operating restrictions in the register must therefore attach directly to the runtime enforcers and site workflows that govern daily operation. Cryptographically signed speed envelopes constrain the onboard permission path, while scheduled maintenance intervals bind facility operations. Release pipelines check that both controls are active in the authorized configuration, while runtime monitors enforce only the physical bounds that onboard sensors can read.

Model retraining or software updates must inherit every open restriction whose closure test has not yet passed. Release gates must check the signed operating limits and the status of their supporting evidence before authorizing a deployment. Dropping to a restricted mode requires its own validated safety case; an open register entry does not automatically clear a slower mode for service. At runtime, low-level controllers enforce speed ceilings and actuator limits, while facility crews maintain floor traction and conveyor loading rules at the scheduled intervals.

A restriction can also lapse in the middle of a motion, when a signed envelope expires, an inspection interval runs out while the base is in a route, or a deployment gate withdraws a mode. The lapse must hand over to the fallback ladder rather than to a generic stop. A trajectory chunk already executing inside the old restriction may finish only if its lease, state estimate, envelope, and supporting physical premises remain valid through completion. Withdrawing permission for new proposals can preserve an already admitted chunk under those conditions; loss of a premise that supports its execution cannot. Losing active permission altogether demands a physically feasible stop within the remaining budget. The rung must match the state, because controlled deceleration, holding brakes at zero velocity, torque removal, and full power isolation produce different transients. No register entry can replace those physical distinctions with a single emergency-stop primitive.

↩︎ The ladder of state-matched fallback responses, from minimal projection to complete torque removal, is established in The Fallback Ladder.

New sensors can justify wider operating envelopes only after they pass empirical qualification. Where real-time detection is impractical, the register must rely on a conservative action, passive containment, periodic proof testing, or a restricted operating domain (the responses to a hidden state that section 18.3.4.2 develops). Choosing among those responses requires comparing the distance at which a detector could spot the hazard against the deadline for committed motion.

18.3.4 Failures without detectors

An automated floor-film detector can retire the surface-friction restriction only if its coverage, false-alarm rate, and detection latency fit inside the route’s stopping budget rather than a standalone model benchmark. In the known-component budget, running at the candidate aisle speed leaves 131.0 mm of physical margin, which corresponds to an allowable delay of 100.8 ms. Setting aside 63.3 ms for dispatch-to-admission processing leaves 37.5 ms of timing reserve. That reserve is an upper bound on unallocated latency, not a certified budget for new perception pipelines, and it offers no protection against a pedestrian who keeps walking toward the robot during the stop.

A forward-looking fluid detector faces an even tighter spatial deadline. The required detection range, minus the distance the robot travels during sensor integration and neural inference, must cover the full dry-floor stopping distance before the tires reach the leading edge of the film: \[\RequiredDetectionRange \ge \SpeedAtDetection\,\DetectionInterval + \KnownComponentStopDistance(\SpeedAtDetection), \tag{3}\] where \(\KnownComponentStopDistance(\SpeedAtDetection)\) denotes the known-component dry-floor budget of notebook 18.2, the seven rows of The warehouse mobile manipulator's stopping budget. The detection interval \(\DetectionInterval\) covers only the film detector’s own work, from the moment the film enters its range to the moment it reports. That report is the observation that starts the pre-brake chain, so the observation age and admission already inside \(\KnownComponentStopDistance\) follow it rather than overlap it. Where the two do overlap in a real pipeline, adding them is conservative. With a chosen sensing and inference latency of 100 ms at the 1.3 m/s aisle speed, the detector must see the film at least 1.10 m ahead. That range is almost exactly the 1.10 m clear distance, and not by coincidence. The dry budget and the spare together make up the clear distance, and the detector’s 100 ms of travel nearly equals the 100.8 ms that the spare buys. A detector that sees only as far as the clear distance therefore spends the whole timing allowance by itself, leaving nothing for the 63.3 ms dispatch-to-admission allocation. To fit beside that allocation, its latency must stay under the 37.5 ms reserve, or its range must grow. If classification occurs only after the drive wheels enter the fluid, the stopping distance expands to 2.13 m, far beyond the 1.10 m clear distance. A classification received after the wheels cross onto the film can serve incident analysis, but it arrives too late to avert the collision.

Observational indistinguishability and the action deadline

When an oil film and dry concrete produce identical camera and lidar readings until the robot is already inside its dry-floor stopping distance, no classifier running on those sensors can trigger a stop in time. That limit is observational indistinguishability. Consider two physical world states, \(\SafeWorldState\) and \(\HazardousWorldState\). In state \(\SafeWorldState\), a commanded actuator trajectory \(\ControlInput(\TimeInstant)\) runs safely. In state \(\HazardousWorldState\), that same command \(\ControlInput(\TimeInstant)\) causes structural damage or human injury, requiring the permission path to trigger an emergency stop. The constraint governing this stop is distance rather than elapsed time: because braking changes the robot’s speed along its path, the point of harm is a place in the room. Let \(\RemainingHarmDistance\) denote the remaining distance to that boundary when the distinguishing observation arrives. An emergency stop finishes short of the hazard only if: \[\RemainingHarmDistance \ge \SpeedAtDetection\,(\EnforcerComputeTime + \ActuatorResponseTime) + \BrakingDistance(\SpeedAtDetection), \tag{4}\] matching the physical stopping relation used throughout this book. The enforcer’s compute time and the actuator’s response time together act as the pre-brake delay \(\PrebrakeDelay\), counted from the arrival of the distinguishing observation rather than from a fixed capture event, and \(\BrakingDistance(\SpeedAtDetection)\) is the braking distance of the chosen stop. Dividing this distance constraint by the approach speed gives an equivalent observation deadline: \(\DeadlineInstant = \HarmInstant - \EnforcerComputeTime - \ActuatorResponseTime - \BrakingDistance(\SpeedAtDetection)/\SpeedAtDetection\), where \(\HarmInstant\) is the instant the unbraked trajectory would reach impact.
