Because \(\HarmInstant\) marks when the robot would reach the hazard at constant cruising speed \(\SpeedAtDetection\), the quantity \(\HarmInstant - \DeadlineInstant\) expresses the required spatial clearance \(\RemainingHarmDistance\) in units of cruising-speed travel time. Once braking begins, the decelerating robot takes longer than \(\BrakingDistance(\SpeedAtDetection)/\SpeedAtDetection\) to cover the remaining distance, so it safely comes to rest just short of the spatial boundary at a clock instant later than \(\HarmInstant\). A common mistake subtracts the full stopping duration \(\StoppingDuration\) instead of the speed-normalized distance \(\BrakingDistance/\SpeedAtDetection\), treating the robot as if it kept moving at full cruising speed until the instant it came to rest. For the jerk-limited profile from 1.3 m/s, braking lasts 931.2 ms but covers a distance equal to only 465.6 ms of constant-speed travel. Writing the deadline purely in time over-reserves 465.6 ms, or 605.3 mm of clearance, an error larger than the entire 131.0 mm spare. The time form errs on the safe side, but it refuses operating modes that the distance form rightly admits. Manipulator contact follows the same logic: once an end-effector touches a stiff surface, contact force rather than position sets the observation deadline (Intentional contact and in-contact energy barriers).

Charging the full stop time over-reserves several times the entire spare.

The paired worlds in figure 3 separate physical state from observable history. Their common brake command has different consequences, although the depicted channel supplies the same inputs through the action deadline.

A timely separating observation changes this argument only if the machine can obtain it within the same physical budget. When no such observation is available, the permission path must choose an action qualified for both candidate states or withhold the operating mode.

If the sensor stream \(\MeasurementVector(\TimeInstant)\) produced under state \(\SafeWorldState\) matches that under \(\HazardousWorldState\) up to \(\DeadlineInstant\) for every admissible control input, no algorithm acting on those measurements can tell the safe state apart from the hazardous state in time to prevent harm:

Definition 18.1: Observational indistinguishability

Observational indistinguishability is the condition in which two physical states that require different responses produce the same observation history, under every input the machine may admissibly apply, until the deadline by which a state-specific response must begin.

  1. Significance: It turns an observation limit into a stopping-budget violation. The deadline is set by equation 4, so if the film and the dry floor look identical until the remaining distance falls below \(\SpeedAtDetection\,(\EnforcerComputeTime + \ActuatorResponseTime) + \BrakingDistance(\SpeedAtDetection)\), that evidence cannot justify continuing the dry-floor mode. The machine must instead use a response already qualified for both states or withhold that mode.
  2. Distinction: Noise and weak detectors obscure information that more data or a better model can recover; indistinguishability means the information is absent from the channel. Unlike unobservability (definition 9.3), which asks whether a state can ever be reconstructed from the output history, it is tied to a deadline and to the inputs the machine may apply.
  3. Common pitfall: A frequent misconception is that a more capable classifier on the same channel will eventually separate the states. Identical histories give every classifier identical inputs. A designed probe can separate states hidden under the current observation policy only when an admissible excitation reveals a difference before the deadline. If every admissible input leaves the histories identical, the response requires a new channel or an action valid in both states.

↩︎ The observation contract, which bounds the age and error of what a channel reports but cannot certify a state the channel never carries, is established in The Observation Contract.

Hermann, Robert, and Arthur J. Krener. 1977. “Nonlinear Controllability and Observability.” IEEE Transactions on Automatic Control 22 (5): 728–40.

5 Auxiliary Signal Design: The multimodel method computes a small test input that guarantees the normal model and each declared failure model produce different outputs within a fixed test horizon, despite bounded disturbances (Campbell and Nikoukhah 2004). A probe can therefore rule out only the failures someone wrote down as models, which is why the hidden premise must first be named in the register. The input bound and the horizon map onto this chapter’s budgets, since the probe must fit the force envelope and the separation must arrive before \(\DeadlineInstant\).

Campbell, Stephen L., and Ramine Nikoukhah. 2004. Auxiliary Signal Design for Failure Detection. Princeton University Press.

The qualification under every input the machine may admissibly apply matters because whether two states look different often depends on how the machine excites the plant, as shown in nonlinear observability theory (Hermann and Krener 1977). In some settings, an engineer can inject a small, safe control test input that makes the two states produce different sensor readings before the deadline. This technique is active probing: applying a bounded test excitation to expose hidden state differences in the sensor stream before the action deadline, the same excitation that Observability Ceilings and Estimation Limits uses to expose a drifting force-sensor bias. Active probing draws on auxiliary signal design5 from fault detection (Campbell and Nikoukhah 2004). For example, a controlled tug at a qualified proof load can check whether a door latch holds at that load before the arm commits to its full pull, though it does not measure the latch’s remaining yield margin. On the floor, a brief torque pulse at low speed, applied before the base enters a route, makes the wheels slip measurably on a film and not on dry concrete, turning the friction under the tires into telemetry. Such a slip check measures only the patch of floor the wheels already stand on; it cannot see a film ahead, which is why the forward detector above has its own range condition. Active probing costs time and disturbs the motion, and both costs must fit inside the safety budgets. Where no admissible probe can separate the states within the timing budget, the design must fall back on conservative actions or passive mechanical containment.

Responses when the state remains hidden

Active probing is the first of five design responses when passive runtime observations leave candidate physical states indistinguishable. When an admissible excitation can separate the states before the deadline, active probing supplies the missing observation online; if every admissible input leaves them indistinguishable, the remaining four responses must carry the claim without online separation. Proof testing measures the state from outside the runtime channel at scheduled intervals, such as an unloaded tare of a sensor or a load test of a latch, and covers the time between tests by argument. A conservative action chooses a command safe in both candidate states, such as a speed low enough for the film. When control commands alone cannot bound the interaction, passive containment limits the physical consequence mechanically, such as a compliant element that caps contact force whatever the software believes. A restricted operational domain withholds authority altogether from states that cannot be told apart, such as routes without a spill procedure. The register entries above already used the conservative action and the restricted domain, and active probing was shown at the latch and the floor. The wrist sensor and the latch below show proof testing and passive containment at work on the running machine.

Sensor drift is the slow form of the same barrier. The wrist strain gauge of Observability Ceilings and Estimation Limits, whose bias wanders as thermal cycles and mechanical stress relaxation alter its bridge resistance, reports only the sum of contact force and bias. A drifting bias and a real change in force therefore produce the same reading and a zero innovation. What this chapter adds is the choice of response. Every contact threshold that depends on that signal inherits the drift, and no response chosen at runtime can see it. The response must therefore be proof testing, meaning an unloaded tare or a proof-test fixture at an interval short enough that the drift accumulated between tests stays inside the threshold’s margin.

↩︎ Mechanical degradation and thermal calibration drift as continuing obligations of a release verdict are established in Degradation, drift, and observation cadence.

6 Proof Test: Functional safety defines a proof test as a periodic test that reveals dangerous failures no online diagnostic can detect, so that the function can be restored to its as-new condition (International Electrotechnical Commission 2010). A hidden failure that arrives at a constant rate \(\ComponentFailureRate\) is present, on average over a test interval \(\Time\), for a fraction of about \(\ComponentFailureRate \Time/2\) of the time when \(\ComponentFailureRate \Time\) is small. The interval is therefore itself a safety parameter, because it bounds how long a weakened latch can go unseen.

International Electrotechnical Commission. 2010. IEC 61508:2010: Functional Safety of Electrical/Electronic/Programmable Electronic Safety-Related Systems. International Electrotechnical Commission.

A weakened door latch poses the same observability barrier on a millisecond timescale. Before the metal yields, joint torque sensors read the same reaction force against a fatigued latch as against a sound one, so the torque channel carries no information about how much yield margin remains. Because plastic yield is permanent, no software command sent after the latch bends can undo the damage, and running a faster classifier on joint torque signals adds zero protection. A credible safety strategy must bring in information from outside the runtime torque channel. Scheduled proof tests6 check that the latch survives a specified proof load at fixed intervals, without measuring its ultimate yield strength. Even so, proof testing holds only if maintenance records show that fatigue, wear, and corrosion cannot push the latch’s yield strength below the arm’s contact ceiling between tests.

Adding a passive compliant element to the structure can mechanically cap interaction forces across a qualified deflection stroke, the principle behind series elastic actuators (Pratt and Williamson 1995) and mechanical torque limiters. Two physical conditions govern compliant load paths, both set by the stroke the arm actively drives after contact detection rather than the total uncompressed spring length. When a contact tripwire \(\ContactTripwireForce\) triggers, the arm keeps moving during its controller response latency \(\ContactResponseLatency\) before decelerating at rate \(\ArmStoppingDeceleration\), driving the mechanism through a post-contact stroke of \(\PostContactDrivenStroke = \SpeedAtDetection\,\ContactResponseLatency + \SpeedAtDetection^2/(2\ArmStoppingDeceleration)\). To keep the contact force below the declared strength floor \(\LatchStrengthFloor\) (a lower bound on the latch’s strength, unrelated to the floor friction), the equivalent stiffness \(\EquivalentSeriesStiffness\) of the combined series load path (compliant element and strike plate) must satisfy equation 5: \[\ContactTripwireForce + \EquivalentSeriesStiffness\,\PostContactDrivenStroke \le \LatchStrengthFloor \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad \EquivalentSeriesStiffness \le \frac{\LatchStrengthFloor-\ContactTripwireForce}{\SpeedAtDetection\,\ContactResponseLatency + \SpeedAtDetection^2/(2\ArmStoppingDeceleration)}. \tag{5}\] In addition, the compliant element’s allowable stroke must exceed its deflection under the peak allowable force, \((\LatchStrengthFloor-\CompliantElementPreload)/\CompliantElementStiffness\), so the spring never bottoms out against a hard stop. The threshold \(\LatchStrengthFloor\) is not the latch’s actual yield strength, which remains unobserved between proof tests; it is a chosen lower strength bound recorded as an assumption in the register until physical evidence supports it. Accountable judgment chooses the design bound; it cannot create material strength.

Pratt, Gill A., and Matthew M. Williamson. 1995. “Series Elastic Actuators.” Proceedings of the 1995 IEEE/RSJ International Conference on Intelligent Robots and Systems (IROS) 1: 399–406. https://doi.org/10.1109/IROS.1995.525827.

The warehouse door latch shows what passive compliance buys. During a guarded approach at 0.03 m/s, meeting a stiff strike plate of stiffness 4.0 × 10⁵ N/m satisfies equation 5 with a required arm deceleration of 2.95 m/s², matching the guarded latch stop derived in Intentional contact and in-contact energy barriers. At a faster approach speed of 0.10 m/s, however, the same stiff contact would require an impossible arm deceleration of 400 m/s², forcing the permission path to refuse the trajectory. Holding arm deceleration to the guarded stopping bound instead drives the arm through 1.89 mm after it crosses the 15 N tripwire, capping the allowable series stiffness at 44.9 kN/m. Because series compliance combines inversely (\(1/\EquivalentSeriesStiffness = 1/\CompliantElementStiffness + 1/\LatchPlateStiffness\)), pairing the compliant element with the strike plate requires a spring stiffness of at most 50.5 kN/m and an available stroke of at least 2.0 mm to stay within the 100 N design force.

This kinematic calculation assumes a stiff position-tracking drive that enforces the commanded \(\ArmStoppingDeceleration\) deceleration profile throughout the post-tripwire stroke regardless of contact reaction. Once spring compression exceeds \(\EffectiveImpactMass\,\ArmStoppingDeceleration\), the passive spring alone would decelerate the \(\EffectiveImpactMass\) effective mass faster than \(\ArmStoppingDeceleration\), so a controller that tracks the gentler kinematic ramp keeps pushing forward into the spring until the stroke completes. If the safety response instead cuts motor torque completely, the arm’s effective mass 12 kg coasts into the spring, and energy conservation sets the peak force. A spring compressed to force \(\SpringCompressionForce\) stores potential energy \(\SpringCompressionForce^2/(2\EquivalentSeriesStiffness)\); at the tripwire, the spring already holds \(\ContactTripwireForce^2/(2\EquivalentSeriesStiffness)\), and the coasting mass adds its kinetic energy \(\EffectiveImpactMass{}\SpeedAtDetection^2/2\). The resulting peak impact force is \(\sqrt{\ContactTripwireForce^2 + \EquivalentSeriesStiffness\,\EffectiveImpactMass\,\SpeedAtDetection^2}\), the dynamic impact regime that governs high-speed robot collisions (Haddadin et al. 2009). With the series spring installed, this coasting peak is 74.9 N, below the 100 N that the braked case reaches. That result is not a paradox. The stiffness bound was sized for a drive that holds the arm to the gentle 2.95 m/s² stop and so keeps pushing through the whole 1.89 mm stroke, bringing the force right to the floor. With torque removed, nothing pushes; the spring alone slows the 12 kg effective mass, harder than that commanded stop, and halts it in a shorter stroke at a lower force. Striking the stiff plate without compliance, by contrast, produces peak forces of 219.6 N at the faster approach speed and 67.4 N under the guarded approach. Within the qualified approach-speed and stroke envelope, the compliant element keeps both modeled responses at or below the latch’s declared design strength; driving beyond that stroke would bottom out the spring and break the bound. Even so, passive compliance does not verify latch strength: qualification must still test damping, material fatigue, and structural load paths rather than spring stiffness alone.

Haddadin, Sami, Alin Albu-Schäffer, and Gerd Hirzinger. 2009. “Requirements for Safe Robots: Measurements, Analysis and New Insights.” The International Journal of Robotics Research 28 (11–12): 1507–27. https://doi.org/10.1177/0278364909343970.

A series spring keeps the coasting peak under the declared 100 N floor.

The door latch, the oil film, and the packaging tab pose the same problem. When a safety premise cannot be evaluated before an execution deadline, the first question is whether an admissible probe can separate the states in time. If it can, active probing supplies the missing observation; otherwise proof testing, a conservative action, passive containment, or a restricted operational domain must support the claim. The choice among them depends on the claim each response can support and the evidence needed to maintain it, and every such mitigation must also state the evidence that would retire it.

Checkpoint 18.2: Premises no sensor reports

The warehouse machine’s hardest limits are premises its own telemetry cannot check, and each one needs an entry, a restriction, and a response chosen before the deadline.

18.4 Choosing the Next Capability

Pouring engineering effort into a faster proposer when an unmeasured floor or an unseated tab actually limits the cell leaves the operating restriction in place. In a deployed packaging cell, restricted transit speeds and human-supervised sign-offs call for different investments. Speeding up proposal generation improves throughput inside the current safety envelope, whereas qualifying a new contact or engagement sensor can retire the need for human sign-off altogether. Each advance requires two deliverables: a predeclared task metric and a physical test of the operating premise it changes. Tuning execution inside an existing envelope and gathering the evidence to widen that envelope are distinct engineering programs, and the register’s open entries decide which one the next capability needs.

18.4.1 Match the experiment to the missing evidence

Treating every open entry in the residual-claims register by training another neural detector fails whenever the missing evidence is a field evaluation, a mathematical bound, or a fallback that the body cannot execute. The open entries do not all call for the same kind of work; each falls into one of four gap classes, and the class, not the entry’s urgency, decides which experiment can close it:

  • Unmeasured observables require dedicated sensors that can tell the relevant physical states apart before the action deadline passes.
  • Evaluation deficits require randomized field trials or statistical estimators that represent the actual operating population while accounting for correlated samples and rare tail events.
  • Theoretical deficits require bounded analytical arguments whose physical premises can be proved mathematically or tested on the actual hardware plant.
  • Scope mismatches require changing the system architecture or narrowing the operational design domain.

Naming the exact gap class directs the next experiment to the evidence a register entry actually lacks, turning the open premises in the claims register into distinct work programs. Undetected floor films, hidden cross-aisle pedestrians, ambiguous object identities, and unseated packaging tabs are unmeasured observables. Each calls for either a qualified sensor channel with a tested miss bound or an operating restriction that avoids the hazard, such as the downstream manual inspection that stands in for the tab sensor. Loaded dispatch-to-admission timing requires a supported timing bound under declared workload and resource limits, challenged by loaded and adversarial latency measurements. By contrast, the coverage factor \(\ShieldInterceptionCoverage\) for a safety shield requires statistical qualification on the declared population of hazardous proposals, whereas finite stress tests alone cannot establish a worst-case timing bound. Gaps between formal plant models and physical hardware are theoretical deficits, requiring robust control bounds. A scope mismatch differs from all three because no measurement, trial, or proof inside the current architecture can fix it. Claiming a de-energized safe stop for a balancing biped is the clearest example: cutting torque is itself the hazard, so the remedy is a different fallback architecture or a narrower claim, as section 18.4.3 discusses.

Statistical extreme value theory bounds rare tail exceedances only when its asymptotic and independence assumptions hold; fitting a parametric curve to test data cannot validate unmodeled contact physics or reveal unobserved floor friction. Similarly, Hamilton-Jacobi reachability analysis yields invariant sets only for an explicitly declared plant and disturbance bound. Moving an unverified friction assumption from field testing into a rigid-body simulator or an analytical proof leaves it just as unverified as before.

↪ Extreme value theory and its asymptotic and independence assumptions are developed in Extreme Value Theory and Tail Risk.

↪ Hamilton-Jacobi reachability and its declared disturbance model are developed in Hamilton-Jacobi reachability and worst-case differential games.

18.4.2 What a restriction costs

Knowing which experiment would close an entry does not say whether running it is worth the cost. An entry in the residual-claims register can buy protection at a throughput cost. On the warehouse machine, the oil-film restriction lowers cruising speed along the aisle from 1.3 m/s to 1 m/s, imposing a 23.1 percent speed penalty. At steady cruise, this derating stretches transit time across each 30 m aisle segment from 23.1 s to 30 s. That single constraint adds 30 percent more transit time per leg before accounting for acceleration profiles and docking maneuvers. Because transit time scales as \(1/\SpeedAtDetection\), the percentage increase in leg time exceeds the percentage cut in speed.

An operational restriction remains in force until its closure test passes. That test carries a tangible empirical cost: the 2,995 seeded-film physical trials specified in the register, for example. The separate claim about safety shield coverage requires 29,956 seeded hazardous proposals; that campaign does not close the film entry. The claims register therefore confronts systems engineers with an economic optimization problem alongside the technical one. On throughput savings alone, a formal closure campaign pays back when the cumulative value recovered exceeds the combined cost of the test campaign and the sensing instrument it qualifies. A full investment decision must also account for changes in exposure, maintenance, and the value of newly supported tasks. An operational restriction can remain the economical choice when its physical premise can be maintained reliably at low cost, such as through qualified cleaning and spill-control procedures along a few pallet-jack routes. Evaluating this trade-off requires site-specific parameters: the economic value of a completed task, the machine’s active duty hours on restricted routes, and the marginal expense of executing controlled test trials. In the absence of proprietary site economics, time provides the universal benchmark that transfers directly across facilities.

The same operational accounting governs fleet deployments. When commercial logistics facilities contract bipedal humanoids under robots-as-a-service agreements (GXO Logistics 2024), commercial performance is measured by volume moved, 100,000 totes at an initial customer facility by late 2025 (Agility Robotics 2025). In these operational environments, physical interventions, speed restrictions, and withheld autonomy modes can reduce completed-task throughput; their billing effect depends on the service contract. The gating criteria established during release validation translate directly into ongoing operating expenses.

GXO Logistics. 2024. GXO Signs Industry-First Multi-Year Agreement with Agility Robotics. Press release, 27 June 2024.
Agility Robotics. 2025. Digit Moves over 100,000 Totes in Commercial Deployment. Agility Robotics, 20 November 2025.
Bainbridge, Lisanne. 1983. “Ironies of Automation.” Automatica 19 (6): 775–79. https://doi.org/10.1016/0005-1098(83)90046-8.

Intervention rates incur a double cost. Frequent human takeovers consume costly labor hours and tether fleet operations to local technician availability. Conversely, rare interventions introduce the classic ironies of automation: an operator who seldom intervenes struggles to maintain the situational awareness and manual dexterity needed when an emergency takeover occurs (Bainbridge 1983). A defensible business case therefore reports proposer autonomy, whole-machine task success, and protective responses separately, as the first fallacy in section 18.5 insists, because each carries a different price.

↩︎ The labor a deployment moves onto technicians, sweep crews, and remote assistants, and the uses of their data it bars, are established in The Labor the Machine Moves.

18.4.3 Research that changes an engineering result

The oil-film campaign can be priced because the closure test itself is already known: run 2,995 paired braking trials on a representative spill or qualify an online probe. Other open entries in a register cannot be closed by scheduling a standard test campaign because no engineering method yet supplies the missing evidence. In physical AI, research warrants industry and academic investment when its resolution improves useful task performance within justified limits or supplies support for a broader operating claim. Six central challenges define this research frontier as of 2026 (table 3); each stresses one of the four laws, falls into one gap class of section 18.4.1, and names the form of evidence that would count as progress.

Generalist and cross-embodiment policies stress the fourth law, permission must be earned, because evidence gathered on one body and one model version must now earn permission for another. A single pretrained vision-language-action (VLA) foundation model can now control diverse kinematics. For example, flow-matching architectures have been trained across single-arm, dual-arm, and mobile manipulation platforms from a unified model (Black et al. 2024), using datasets from different embodiments. Modern pipelines continuously fine-tune from production deployments via reinforcement learning with human interventions (Physical Intelligence et al. 2025) or remap learned trajectories across distinct kinematic morphologies (Gemini Robotics Team 2025).

↩︎ Why demonstrations logged on one embodiment carry its dynamics and do not transfer unchanged to another is established in Cross-Embodiment Transfer.

However, empirical safety evidence compiled on a dual-arm cobot cannot be assumed to hold when that same policy executes on a mobile manipulator. Furthermore, policies that continually adapt in production require reopening the release claims whose supporting evidence depends on the changed behavior (Maintaining Release Through Updates). The enduring challenge is establishing formal transfer guarantees across both physical embodiments and continuous model updates. Concrete progress requires rigorous transfer arguments with explicit kinematic and dynamic equivalence conditions, verifiable by predicting a new morphology’s closed-loop failure distributions before physical deployment. The cross-embodiment approach also reinforces the necessity of an independent permission pipeline: physical protection limits are calibrated to each specific mechanical chassis and remain invariant regardless of how frequently upstream generalist weights are updated.

Data scaling is the empirical frontier of the second law, endogenous data. Large-scale empirical studies encompassing more than 40,000 real-robot teleoperated demonstrations indicate that out-of-distribution generalization across novel environments and visual scenes follows an approximate power law governed by environmental diversity, whereas collecting redundant demonstrations within identical scenes shows sharp diminishing returns (Lin et al. 2025). In physical systems, structural diversity rather than raw sample count dictates data utility.

The open challenge is determining whether these empirical scaling laws generalize to contact-rich manipulation, long-horizon multi-step planning, and safety-critical edge states. In embodied settings, an agent’s closed-loop control actions actively determine its subsequent perceptual distributions, concentrating risk in rare, unmodeled corner cases. Meaningful progress requires publishing verified scaling curves that measure tail-risk closed-loop failure rates within declared operational design domains (ODDs), replacing benchmark evaluations that measure only average-case task completion rates.

↩︎ Why the diversity of pooled demonstrations, not their raw count, governs their value to a target task is established in Choosing pooled experience for the target task.

Generative world models also stress the fourth law, because they offer a way around the physical exposure wall only if their rollouts count as evidence. Latent dynamics formulations and high-fidelity video generation models increasingly act as general-purpose physics simulators (NVIDIA et al. 2025; Assran et al. 2025). Researchers have begun deploying these generative models directly as policy evaluators, demonstrating strong correlation with real-robot task execution on benchmark tasks (Jeon et al. 2026). Synthetic rollouts execute at large parallel scale for a fraction of the cost of physical hardware hours.

Nevertheless, strict evidentiary limits constrain synthetic evaluation. A learned world model may estimate latent physical variables, but its training fit does not validate those estimates in unobserved regimes or justify extrapolating friction discontinuities or structural compliance omitted from its training distribution. The central research question is establishing when synthetic evaluation in a learned world model constitutes valid evidentiary support for real-world release claims. Genuine progress requires formalizing proxy-transfer bounds (Simulation as a conditional proxy) validated against the empirical tail of real-world physical failures rather than aggregate nominal success, accompanied by end-to-end dataset and architecture provenance tracking.

↩︎ Provenance records for learned world models, binding architecture, checkpoint, and dataset lineage, are established in Learned world models as simulators.

7 Fail-Passive and Fail-Operational: Avionics separates a fail-passive function, whose failure causes no significant deviation of path or attitude and hands the task back to the crew, from a fail-operational one, which keeps performing through a failure. Removing torque from a wheeled base is fail-passive in this sense only after it reaches a qualified, mechanically restrained rest. A balancing biped in mid-stride has no such resting state, so its posture control must remain operational until the machine reaches a supported rest, and the certifiable reaction must be a controlled one.

Dynamically stable machines, such as bipedal humanoids and high-speed quadrupeds, are the most stringent test of the first law, irreversibility. As highlighted in perspective 4.2, perspective 7.2, perspective 13.1, and perspective 16.1, the entire architectural chain of proposal, permission, and admission applies directly to balancing robots, with one catastrophic exception: fallback safe states. For the wheeled platform or stationary manipulator considered here, the fallback first reaches a qualified, mechanically restrained standstill before removing drive torque. On a balancing biped in mid-stride, cutting motor power induces an unrecoverable ballistic fall, transforming the safety mechanism into the primary hazard. Instead of bringing the system to an immediate stop, a balancing machine must execute dynamic recovery maneuvers: taking a stabilizing step, initiating a controlled crouch, or executing an active powered descent to a mechanically supported resting posture. Traditional functional safety standards, however, were built around fail-passive de-energization (the torque-removal rungs of the fallback ladder, which The four rungs of the fallback ladder treats as fail-safe only for a plant already at a mechanically restrained standstill). This created what safety researchers term the fail-passive gap7 (Ding et al. 2026). Bridging this divide requires certifying active closed-loop balance stabilization as a safety-rated critical function. Demonstrating meaningful progress requires designing certifiable dynamic reaction chains, analogous to automotive emergency braking profiles. These chains must reliably steer the robot from each qualified gait phase, under the declared fault conditions, into a stable, supported configuration within bounded peak power and kinetic energy budgets. The most capable learned machines are thus the ones for which a safe stop is hardest to define.

Contact-rich, dexterous manipulation centers on physical AI’s canonical unobserved state: high-frequency contact interaction. Determining whether a snap-fit latch yields, a packaging tab seats, or a fragile container begins to crush cannot be reliably deduced from joint motor currents or external RGB-D cameras alone. High-resolution tactile sensing provides a direct channel for several of these contact dynamics; each claimed observation still needs qualification for its materials, geometry, and deadline. Modern vision-based elastomeric tactile sensors directly capture surface deformation fields, contact geometry, and distributed shear stresses at the end-effector (Yuan et al. 2017).

↩︎ Optical elastomer tactile sensing and its timing and calibration obligations are established in Tactile contact sensing.

Tedrake, Russ. 2023. Robotic Manipulation: Perception, Planning, and Control. Massachusetts Institute of Technology.

Closing the loop on contact, however, challenges the third law, proposal is not permission. Contact forces escalate within milliseconds of initial impact, far outstripping the round-trip latency of high-level neural planners and placing strict burdens on low-level passive or series elastic compliance (section 18.3.4.2). The open engineering challenge is integrating tactile feedback into the formal permission path. Concrete progress requires qualifying tactile perception channels with rigorous statistical miss rates for incipient slip and shear yield, coupled with low-latency impedance controllers whose force limits can be deterministically verified and bounded by safety hardware (Tedrake 2023).

Certifying learned models that directly implement safety functions is the central challenge of the fourth law. Although such components are assigned the notified-body route of The standards map for the running machine, the robotics industry lacks standardized methodologies to certify deep neural networks at automotive or industrial safety integrity levels (SIL) or PL, because current verification techniques face stark limitations. Exact satisfiability modulo theories (SMT)-based and mixed-integer linear programming verification tools can establish formal input-output properties over declared neural network weights (Katz et al. 2017), yet these proofs remain vulnerable to open-world modeling gaps outside the declared plant and observations. Purely statistical validation collides with the exposure wall, requiring billions of physical test cycles to establish low target failure probabilities. Runtime safety filters and shield wrappers transfer the verification burden onto the shield’s own coverage and latency envelope (Hsu et al. 2024). The unresolved systems challenge is constructing hybrid safety arguments: composing analytical proofs where deterministic physics models are tractable with statistical bounds where perceptual models operate, and allocating risk budgets across both tiers. Until such composite certification arguments mature, the architecture developed here keeps learned proposers outside the safety-critical permission path. Verifiable progress requires publishing complete, audited conformity dossiers for learned safety components alongside their formal assurance cases.

Hsu, Kai-Chieh, Haimin Hu, and Jaime F. Fisac. 2024. “The Safety Filter: A Unified View of Safety-Critical Control in Autonomous Systems.” Annual Review of Control, Robotics, and Autonomous Systems 7: 47–72. https://doi.org/10.1146/annurev-control-071723-102940.

Two systems challenges cut across all six problems. The first is continual online adaptation, which turns the model into a moving target. Whether a proposer retrains asynchronously on aggregated fleet telemetry or adapts its latent state online to accommodate component wear and shifting terrain (Kumar et al. 2021), background model updates must never starve the permission pipeline of compute or bus bandwidth. The second is multi-robot fleet coordination, which couples otherwise independent permission boundaries. Transmitting shared task intents across lossy wireless links cannot substitute for the local, hard-real-time sensor streams required by an individual robot’s admission gate. Physically cooperative manipulation of a shared payload likewise couples actuator contact dynamics directly across platforms. Across all of these problems, advancing physical AI does not require abandoning established systems rigor; it requires extending it. Unlocking the next capability begins by isolating a concrete physical task that the current system cannot safely execute, identifying the missing empirical premise or unmeasured observable, and engineering the precise evidential argument required to justify expanding the machine’s operational envelope.

Kumar, Ashish, Zipeng Fu, Deepak Pathak, and Jitendra Malik. 2021. “RMA: Rapid Motor Adaptation for Legged Robots.” Robotics: Science and Systems XVII. https://doi.org/10.15607/RSS.2021.XVII.011.

18.5 Fallacies and Pitfalls

When engineers move physical AI systems such as the warehouse mobile manipulator or the packaging cell from isolated benchmarks to production floors, the traps that remain are conceptual. Every local calculation can succeed numerically while the system-level reasoning fails, because the team has misread how physical dynamics, learned proposers, and runtime enforcement relate. The traps below recur on both machines.

Fallacy: A protective intervention means the whole machine failed its task.

When a runtime safety governor or control barrier filter modifies an actuator command, engineers often mistakenly log the episode as a system-level failure. In practice, a least-change speed correction or torque clamp often allows an autonomous mobile robot or packaging arm to complete its physical mission smoothly within safe boundaries. If the warehouse machine enters a route whose floor is inspected only to \(\CoulombFrictionCoefficient \ge\) 0.12 and the governor trims its speed from the 1.3 m/s aisle speed to the register’s 1 m/s restricted mode for that floor, the delivery still completes. The leg takes 30 percent longer.

Treating every safety intervention as a mission failure corrupts operational telemetry. Conflating proposer interventions with unrecoverable task aborts conceals where engineering effort should be directed. Development teams must track three metrics independently: raw proposer autonomy (the fraction of time the learned model operates without override), whole-machine task success (whether the physical objective was achieved within operational deadlines), and protective intervention frequency. Decoupling these signals reveals whether system bottlenecks stem from policy distribution shift, sensor latency in the perception pipeline, or overly conservative governor tuning.

↩︎ Proposer autonomy, task success, and protective intervention as separately recorded outcome channels are established in What a Physical Success Rate Measures.

Pitfall: Treating a restriction as evidence that its physical premise holds.

Deriving a tighter operational limit does not guarantee that the physical environment satisfies the assumptions behind that limit. The register’s restricted mode for the warehouse machine, 1 m/s instead of 1.3 m/s, provides a stopping margin only on floors that hold \(\CoulombFrictionCoefficient \ge\) 0.12. Under the clear film, where \(\CoulombFrictionCoefficient\) falls to 0.05 and the available deceleration to 0.49 m/s², the lower speed setpoint carries no stopping guarantee at all; the restriction rests on the same unmeasured premise as the mode it replaced.

Digital artifacts cannot substitute for physical friction. Neither a signed configuration parameter in software, nor a clear path reported by an RGB camera, proves that the ground provides adequate traction. Vision pipelines readily mistake transparent oil or thin ice for dry concrete. A restricted envelope therefore needs its own premise kept true, through the inspection and spill procedure the register assigns to facility operations or through a qualified forward film detector. Monitoring wheel slip and inertial deceleration can reveal, after the fact, that a premise failed, and should trigger the restriction for later motion, but it cannot rescue the stop already under way. A safety envelope is only as credible as the physical premise beneath it.

Fallacy: More model capacity or simulated exposure settles every residual claim.

Scaling up neural network parameter counts or running billions of simulated training steps improves policy interpolation across modeled conditions, but neither technique can overcome an unmodeled physical reality. A deep learning policy cannot infer an unobserved physical state, such as internal material stress, micro-slip at a fingertip, or variable ground compliance, if the sensor suite lacks the transducers to measure it. Similarly, physics simulators rely on simplified approximations of rigid-body contact, Coulomb friction, and non-deformable geometry. Generating large synthetic datasets within a simulator that omits real contact mechanics merely produces policies optimized for fictitious physical dynamics.

When an embodied system displays persistent edge-case failures, the solution rarely lies in adding more transformer layers or randomizing synthetic textures. Engineers must diagnose the specific physical claim that lacks empirical grounding. Resolving the defect requires choosing the appropriate engineering intervention: integrating a dedicated physical sensor to observe the missing state, introducing passive mechanical compliance to absorb unmodeled impact energy, constraining the operational design domain, or calibrating friction models against instrumented hardware trials.

Pitfall: Charging the full stop time against an action deadline.

An engineer who sizes the latest useful observation by subtracting the whole braking duration treats the machine as if it held its speed until it stopped instantly. For the jerk-limited stop from the 1.3 m/s aisle speed, braking lasts 931.2 ms but covers only the distance of 465.6 ms of travel at constant speed. The time form therefore over-reserves 465.6 ms, or 605.3 mm, more than the entire 131.0 mm spare.

The error is safe in the narrow sense that it never admits an unsafe stop, which is why it survives review. Its cost appears elsewhere. A deadline computed this way refuses operating modes the distance form correctly admits, and it hides how much timing allowance a new detector or a slower admission stage can still spend. The deadline belongs to the remaining distance, equation 4, with the braking term divided by speed, never to the stop’s duration.

Fallacy: A safety shield makes the proposer’s error rate irrelevant.

A shield attenuates hazardous proposals; it does not erase them. In notebook 18.3, a full release cycle of adjudicated proposals with no hazardous event still bounds the proposal rate only at 1.387 × 10⁻⁵ \(\text{h}^{-1}\). A shield with interception coverage 0.9999 then leaves a modeled residual of 1.387 × 10⁻⁹ \(\text{h}^{-1}\), above the 1 × 10⁻⁹ \(\text{h}^{-1}\) target even with the common-cause term set to zero. Meeting the target at that proposal bound would need coverage of 0.9999279, and showing the assumed coverage alone takes 29,956 seeded hazardous proposals with no miss.

The residual of equation 2 is a product, so both factors need evidence, and a common-cause failure that blinds the shield in the same states where the proposer errs lowers coverage exactly where it matters. The shield is the reason the proposer need not be perfect, not a reason to stop measuring how often it errs.

Pitfall: Qualifying a forward detector by its miss rate alone.

A floor-film detector that passes 2,995 failure-free seeded trials has a miss bound of 0.1 percent, and a team may treat that result as closing the register entry. Detection that arrives too late is a miss in every sense that matters to the stop. At the 1.3 m/s aisle speed with a 100 ms sensing and inference latency, equation 3 requires the detector to see the film 1.10 m ahead, about the whole 1.10 m clear distance. At that range the latency leaves nothing for the 63.3 ms dispatch-to-admission allocation. To fit beside it, the detector’s latency must stay under the 37.5 ms reserve.

A detector that reports only once the wheels are on the film faces a 2.13 m stop, turning a statistically accurate classification into an unrecoverable overrun. The closure test must therefore predeclare range, latency, false-alarm rate, and the friction assumed after detection, alongside the miss rate, and qualify them together on the routes the restriction covers.

18.6 Summary

The warehouse mobile manipulator now carries one connected design argument, and the packaging cell shows that the same argument transfers to a machine with different mechanics, rates, and hazards. The book opened by asking why a better prediction can still produce a worse physical action, and the chapter’s answer is concrete. A neural network optimizes expected statistical loss over its training distribution, but the physical plant operates under continuous Newtonian mechanics, electromagnetism, and thermodynamics. A better proposer cannot supply the friction premise that an unobserved oil film removes, because no input it receives measures the tire-ground contact interface. It cannot make a derated operating mode evaluated, because the operational restriction that shortens the stop also displaces the proposer into physical configurations its training and evaluation never sampled. It cannot close a detectorless claim such as the glare-hidden packaging tab, because the physical sensor required to resolve that state does not exist on the machine. Nor can a faster forward pass recover the stopping distance already consumed while the vehicle cruised under unguided momentum (\(\BlindTravelDistance = \SpeedAtDetection \PrebrakeDelay\)). In each case, the physical outcome is judged by dynamics, timing, and environmental evidence that the learned prediction does not contain.

The learned model must therefore be engineered as an unprivileged component of a machine, with the rest of the machine supplying what the model lacks, one law at a time. The first law, irreversibility, turns the body’s kinetic, actuator, and thermal limits into budgets that bound any proposal, and the friction floor showed that a single unmeasured term can void those budgets. The second law, endogenous data, confines competence to the states the closed loop actually visits, which is why the packaging cell’s restricted mode opened a regime its evaluation never sampled. The third law, proposal is not permission, places an independent permission path between proposal and actuator, and the shield-dilution bound showed that this path improves a hazard-rate claim only for the hazards it can detect and intercept in time. The fourth law, permission must be earned, grants the machine only what its evidence supports, and the residual-claims register gives each unobserved premise a restriction, an owner, and a predeclared closure test. Table 4 collects the relations the chapter applied to both machines, what each bounds or decides, and where the book derives it.

Key Takeaways: Engineering a machine that senses and acts
  • Useful service joins the four laws: Body limits, learned competence, independent permission, and maintained evidence must support the same task; a better prediction strengthens only one link of that argument.
  • Competence can grow: New qualified experience, retraining, and regression evaluation can extend what the next candidate can justify.
  • Admission can preserve success: A minimally corrected proposal can complete useful work; proposer autonomy, task success, and protection require separate measures.
  • Restrictions need evidence: A lower speed still needs traction, clearance, and a feasible response. A stipulated supported branch teaches the release decision without turning illustrative records into measurements.
  • Residual limits select the next experiment: Missing observations, evaluation support, proof premises, and task scope call for different engineering responses.

The chain from body limits to a release decision can be carried through for any machine, but the chapter’s last lesson lies where that chain runs out. The running machine’s hardest limits were not its slowest computation or its weakest model; they were premises no sensor reports, a floor’s friction and a latch’s strength. Engineering judgment at that edge means naming such a premise, restricting the machine until evidence arrives, and choosing the next measurement, learning experiment, architectural change, or narrower domain by which open premise it retires. The Causal Boundary promised the ability to design, teach, integrate, evaluate, and maintain such a machine within justified operating limits, and to name, when the evidence is incomplete, what would support the next capability. That ability to build, verify, and justify a machine that senses and acts is the enduring discipline this book develops.

What’s Next: From physical AI to the systems discipline
The next unfamiliar machine will bring a different body, task, and environment. Its engineering begins by tracing how observations become proposals and how admitted commands become force, motion, and heat. The methods developed here provide a way to teach useful behavior, integrate it with timely physical control, and determine which operating claims the resulting evidence supports. This work is dedicated to the students, researchers, and systems engineers who take up the challenge of giving machines safe and capable hands in an open physical world.

Prof. Vijay Janapa Reddi, Harvard University
